<Tip>
This reference covers c1i v0.12.0. The CLI is authoritative: run `c1i <command> --help` (or `-h`) for the exact flags, and `c1i docs map` (or `c1i docs map --json`) to print every command path this build accepts, with the family, command, and leaf counts, for a quick comparison.
</Tip>

## Overview of c1i commands

Use this reference to find the command, flags, and output format for any c1i task, from managing identities, apps, and access to policies, automations, and MCP servers.

List and search commands output NDJSON, one JSON object per line. Other commands output formatted JSON. An absent string field in a list row is `null`, not `""`. Mutation commands, such as `apps create`, `requests create grant`, and `tasks approve`, print the API's response unwrapped the same way the matching `get` command unwraps it, so you read an ID with `.id` rather than `.app.id`. Deletes and other empty responses print `{}`; exit `0` means success. A few responses carry a different ID path, such as `.connectorId` for an MCP server. See [Output conventions](/product/cli/c1i#output-conventions) for `auth`, `docs`, and `api` output.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`docs`](#docs) | Explore API documentation and agent guidance (no auth required). |
| [`auth`](#auth) | Manage authentication. |
| [`roles`](#roles) | List and inspect IAM roles (find role IDs for `auth login --scoped-role`). |
| [`users`](#users) | Search, list, and get C1 users, read a user's profile types, and set a delegate. |
| [`apps`](#apps) | Search, create, update, and delete applications, and manage their owners, resource types, resources, routing rules, and usage controls. |
| [`accounts`](#accounts) | List and get application accounts, and set an account's owner. |
| [`attribute-types`](#attribute-types) | List the user attribute types an attribute mapping can target. |
| [`attribute-mappings`](#attribute-mappings) | Map an app's profile attributes onto C1 user fields. |
| [`attributes`](#attributes) | List, search, create, and delete tenant attribute values, such as Compliance Framework and Risk Level. |
| [`entitlements`](#entitlements) | Search, get, create, update, and delete application entitlements, manage their owners, and manage their membership automation, provisioning, and proxy bindings. |
| [`grants`](#grants) | Query access grants, add or remove one directly, and set or clear when a grant is revoked. |
| [`tasks`](#tasks) | Manage access request tasks, including escalation, provisioning retries, and a task's audit trail. |
| [`requests`](#requests) | Create, list, and inspect access requests, including waits, app-account targeting, and action requests. |
| [`connectors`](#connectors) | Manage connectors and their credentials, and diagnose connector syncs. |
| [`bridges`](#bridges) (alias `bridge`) | Manage tunnel bridges, their client credentials, and the services an appliance announces. |
| [`policies`](#policies) | Manage policies (approval, provisioning, and certification workflows). |
| [`access-profiles`](#access-profiles) | Manage access profiles (request catalogs): which entitlements are requestable, and by whom. |
| [`access-reviews`](#access-reviews) | Manage access-review campaigns and templates, their scope, and the reports they generate. |
| [`findings`](#findings) | Manage security findings, the rules that route and transform them, and the tasks that remediate them. |
| [`role-mining`](#role-mining) | Manage role mining analyses, suggestions, and the access profiles generated from them. |
| [`automations`](#automations) | Create, inspect, update, run, and delete automations, and inspect or terminate their executions. |
| [`functions`](#functions) | Inspect, create, update, invoke, and delete C1 functions, and read their commits, invocations, and callers. |
| [`hooks`](#hooks) (alias `hook`) | Manage tool-call interception hooks, which run a function, a built-in pattern, or a JSON patch. |
| [`export`](#export) | Export system log (audit) events, and manage continuous log exporters. |
| [`reports`](#reports) | List, get, update, run, and delete saved reports, and inspect a report's program and a run's provenance. |
| [`upgrade`](#upgrade) (alias `update`) | Check for and install a newer c1i release. |
| [`service-principals`](#service-principals) (aliases `service-principal`, `sp`) | Manage service principals (non-human identities), their client credentials, their subject bindings, and their workload federation trusts. |
| [`workload-federation`](#workload-federation) (alias `wf`) | Manage workload federation providers (external JWT issuers), and evaluate a trust CEL condition against sample claims. |
| [`webhooks`](#webhooks) (alias `webhook`) | Manage outbound webhooks: the https endpoints that policies and automations call. |
| [`settings`](#settings) | Inspect and update tenant settings: AI governance, session security, SSO, cross-app access, contacts, domains, notifications, and the AWS external ID. |
| [`xaa`](#xaa) | Manage cross-app access: resource servers, their scopes and access profiles, and a resource server's client audience mappings. |
| [`mcp gateway`](#mcp-gateway) | Call the C1 MCP gateway directly: list and invoke tools end to end. |
| [`mcp servers`](#mcp-servers) | Register, configure, and inspect MCP servers, including `--wait`. |
| [`mcp tools`](#mcp-tools) | Manage the MCP tools discovered from a server. |
| [`mcp prompts`](#mcp-prompts) | Review and approve the MCP prompts a connector's server exposes. |
| [`mcp resources`](#mcp-resources) | Review and approve the MCP resources a connector's server exposes. |
| [`mcp toolsets`](#mcp-toolsets) | Manage MCP toolsets: requestable bundles of MCP tools. |
| [`mcp bindings`](#mcp-bindings) | Manage which tools belong to which toolset. |
| [`mcp classifiers`](#mcp-classifiers) | Manage MCP gateway AI-governance classifiers. |
| [`api`](#api) | Make raw C1 API requests. |
| [`version`](#version) | Print the c1i version. |
| [`completion`](#completion) | Generate a shell completion script for bash, fish, PowerShell, or zsh. |

## Common workflows

Find the command to start with for a common c1i task.

| Goal | Start with |
| :--- | :--- |
| Request access and wait for the new grant | [`requests create grant`](#requests-create-grant) with `--wait`; re-read the task with [`requests get`](#requests). |
| Approve or deny a request | [`tasks list`](#tasks-list), [`tasks get`](#tasks-get), then [`tasks approve`](#tasks-approve) or [`tasks deny`](#tasks-deny). |
| Grant a manually managed entitlement directly | [`grants add`](#grants-add). This skips request policy and approval. |
| Register and govern an MCP server | [`mcp servers register`](#mcp-servers-register) with `--wait`, [`mcp tools approve`](#mcp-tools-approve), then [`mcp toolsets`](#mcp-toolsets). |
| Promote a connector-discovered app | [`apps managed-state`](#apps-managed-state) after finding the resource type with [`apps resource-types list`](#apps-resource-types). |
| Investigate a connector that isn't syncing | [`connectors sync-status`](#connectors), then [`connectors sync-history`](#connectors) and [`connectors sync-errors`](#connectors). `c1i docs guide diagnose-connector-sync` checks the same ground in order. |
| Register cross-app access for a third-party system | Create the resource server with [`xaa resource-servers create`](#xaa), declare its scopes with [`xaa scopes create`](#xaa), then bundle them with [`xaa access-profiles create`](#xaa). `c1i docs guide configure-cross-app-access` walks through it. |
| Explore C1 docs and API schemas without credentials | [`docs`](#docs): `docs search`, `docs endpoints`, `docs endpoint`, and `docs page`. |
| Find the exact commands a build accepts | `c1i docs map` for the tree and counts; `c1i docs map --json` for full paths and aliases. |

## Global flags

Every command accepts these flags.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--url string` | C1 tenant URL; https required. Precedence: `--url`, `C1I_URL`, then `~/.c1i.yaml`. |
| `--fields string` | Comma-separated JSON fields to keep in read output (dot-paths). Prefer it over `jq` for simple projection. On a mutation command it's a usage error (exit 2), because mutation output is never projected, as it is on the raw reads `mcp gateway call`, `findings settings show`, `findings settings edge show`, and `settings ai-governance tenant-defaults`. |
| `--max-retries int` | Retries for transient API failures (429/5xx); 0 disables (default 4). |
| `--error-format string` | Stderr format: `text` or `json` (default `text`). With `json`, every stderr line is one JSON object (NDJSON). See [Errors and exit codes](#errors-and-exit-codes). |
| `--dry-run` | Preview C1 REST mutations (method, URL, body) without sending them. See [Dry run](#dry-run). `mcp gateway call`, `auth login`, and `auth logout` reject it (exit 2). |
| `--debug` | Trace HTTP requests (method, URL, status, timing) to stderr. |
| `-h`, `--help` | Help for any command. |

Each global flag also reads a `C1I_*` environment variable: `C1I_URL`, `C1I_FIELDS`, `C1I_MAX_RETRIES`, `C1I_ERROR_FORMAT`, `C1I_DRY_RUN`, and `C1I_DEBUG`. A flag on the command line overrides its variable. A non-empty `C1I_DRY_RUN`, `C1I_DEBUG`, `C1I_MAX_RETRIES`, or `C1I_ERROR_FORMAT` value that doesn't parse exits with code 2 before any request, as does a negative `--max-retries`. The same keys in `~/.c1i.yaml` are validated alike.

## Dry run

`--dry-run` (or `C1I_DRY_RUN=1`) previews a C1 REST mutation and sends nothing. The preview is one JSON object on stdout with two keys, `dryRun` and `requests`. `requests` lists each request in send order, with its `method`, full `url`, and the `body` as sent (omitted when the request has no body). A notice on stderr, `dry run: nothing was sent`, accompanies every preview.

```json
{
  "dryRun": true,
  "requests": [
    {
      "method": "POST",
      "url": "https://example.conductor.one/api/v1/task/grant",
      "body": {
        "appEntitlementId": "E1",
        "appId": "A1",
        "identityUserId": "U1"
      }
    }
  ]
}
```

- Credential fields appear as `[redacted]`: fields whose names end in `token`, `secret`, `password`, `signature`, or `apikey`, header values, a URL's username (except `git`) or password, the user of an scp-form git remote (so `ghp_x@github.com:repo.git` prints as `[redacted]@github.com:repo.git`), and secret-named URL parameters. A value that merely looks like an email, such as a display name, prints as sent. A body that isn't JSON prints as `"[redacted: not JSON]"`.
- A preview needs a configured URL (exit 2 without one), and an empty, `.`, or `..` ID is refused with exit 2, as it is for a real request. A preview needs no credentials unless c1i must look something up: a task's current policy step, a hosted MCP server's catalog ID, the caller for `requests create`, a function's commit or current display name, or an app, user, policy, or app account read before sending. Those reads run on a preview too, so the preview needs the matching read access: an unknown app on `apps update`, `apps set-owners`, `apps add-owner`, `apps remove-owner`, or `mcp servers register` exits `4` on a preview, and an app that exists but is soft-deleted exits `2`.
- A preview doesn't prove an ID exists. A command with no read before sending sends no `GET`, so an unknown ID previews at exit `0` and exits `4` for real. To confirm an ID, read it with the family's `get` or `list` command.
- A preview has no `id`. If an inherited `C1I_DRY_RUN=1` turns a script's writes into previews, `jq -r .id` prints `null` and the script carries on. Extract IDs with `jq -er`, which fails on a preview.
- `upgrade --dry-run` follows the same JSON preview shape: `dryRun`, `requests`, and an `upgrade` object with the `--check` fields. `upgrade --fields` is a usage error unless you pass `--check`.

## Errors and exit codes

On failure, c1i writes an error to stderr and exits with a code you can branch on.

| Code | Meaning |
| :--- | :--- |
| `0` | Success. |
| `1` | Unclassified error, or a failed write to stdout (such as a full disk). On a mutation, the change was already applied, so verify before you retry. |
| `2` | Usage error: bad flags or arguments, a blank flag or ID, an empty, `.`, or `..` ID, a malformed `--update-mask` or `--body-file` key, a `--fields` that names no path, matches nothing in the result, or arrives on a command whose output is never projected, a refused redirect, a non-approval `tasks skip-step`, or an API `4xx` other than `401`, `403`, `404`, `408`, `429`, and `499`. |
| `3` | Not authenticated, or the API returned `401` or `403`. A `400`, `401`, or `403` from the token endpoint also exits `3`. |
| `4` | The API returned `404`, or a referenced id was not found by a pre-send check. |
| `5` | The API returned `429`. Back off and retry. |
| `6` | C1 failed: a `5xx`, a redirect chain that never settled, a `200` response whose body isn't JSON (including an unusable token response), a `get` answered `200` without the object it reads, a list page in a shape the command doesn't recognize, or a list command whose pagination cursor stopped advancing. |
| `7` | `mcp gateway call` completed, but the tool reported an error. |
| `8` | A system beyond C1 failed: an unreachable upstream connector or gateway, an unreachable external MCP server, a protocol-level gateway error, or any failure of the documentation site for `docs search` and `docs page`, whatever the HTTP status. |

A token-endpoint failure follows the same classification as other responses: `400`/`401`/`403` exits `3`, `404` exits `4`, `408` exits `1`, `429` exits `5`, and a `5xx` or unusable `200` exits `6`. An unreachable token endpoint is a transport failure (exit `1`), not bad credentials.

Several writes read referenced objects before sending. An unknown referenced id exits `4`; an object that exists but is soft-deleted, or a policy of the wrong type, exits `2`; and a read that answers `200` without the object it asked for exits `6`. An object counts as deleted only when its own `deletedAt` is set, so `null` and `""` mean live. `apps update` refuses a malformed app id as a usage error (exit `2`), because the API answers its update path with a `404`. The same checks run under `--dry-run`, so a preview can need the corresponding read access.

With `--error-format json`, stderr is NDJSON: each line is one object with an `error`, `notice`, `progress`, or `debug` key. Select errors with `jq 'select(.error)'`. API errors include `status`, `method`, `path`, and `body`.

An error from a command that writes also carries `"applied"`: `false` when nothing reached the server, `true` when the change was applied, and `"unknown"` when the request may have reached the server, including when a retry then failed. `mcp tools approve` adds an error object for each failed ID and a summary object with `notAttempted`. A batch can exit non-zero after it applied some changes.

## Docs

Explore C1 API documentation and schemas. These commands don't require authentication.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i docs search <query>` | Search C1 documentation by keyword. |
| `c1i docs page <path>` | Fetch a full documentation page by path. A leading slash or a full documentation URL is also accepted. |
| `c1i docs endpoints [--filter <pattern>]` | List all available API endpoints, optionally only those matching a pattern. |
| `c1i docs endpoint <path>` | Show the full request and response schema for a specific API endpoint. |
| `c1i docs openapi` | Dump the raw OpenAPI spec in YAML format. |
| [`c1i docs map`](#docs-map) | Print the live command tree this binary accepts: families, commands, descriptions, and counts. |
| [`c1i docs guide`](#docs-guide) | Print an embedded, task-oriented runbook for a common workflow. |
| [`c1i docs agents`](#docs-agents) (alias `skill`) | Print agent-facing conventions: output contracts, exit codes, and pagination. |
| [`c1i docs readme`](#docs-readme-and-docs-changelog) | Print the README embedded in the c1i binary: the full reference. |
| [`c1i docs changelog`](#docs-readme-and-docs-changelog) | Print the CHANGELOG embedded in the c1i binary, including migration notes under "Upgrading from" headings. |

`docs endpoints`, `docs endpoint`, and `docs openapi` read the public spec at `https://www.c1.ai/api/openapi.yaml`, which includes the MCP, service principal, and access review operations. c1i caches the spec for 24 hours at `~/.c1i/cache/api-openapi.yaml`. If a fetch fails, c1i serves the stale cache and prints a one-line warning on stderr. Each refresh deletes other files in `~/.c1i/cache/`, so keep your own files elsewhere.

`docs endpoint` projects the returned schema with `--fields` like any other read. A path the spec doesn't have exits with code 2. `docs endpoints --filter` with no match is a real no-match in the spec: it prints no rows and a notice on stderr, and exits `0`.

`docs search` returns up to 10 semantic nearest matches with no relevance threshold, so a nonsense query still returns plausible-looking hits. A hit is not proof a concept exists, and an unexpected hit is not proof the thing you searched for is absent — read the snippet or fetch the page with `docs page`. To test whether an endpoint exists, use `docs endpoints --filter`, which can genuinely return nothing.

`docs search` and `docs page` exit with code 8 when the documentation site fails, whatever the cause: an unreachable site, a non-200 response, an unparseable body, or a redirect loop. With no cached spec to fall back on, a redirect loop on `docs openapi`, `docs endpoints`, or `docs endpoint` also exits 8.

### `docs map`

Print every command the binary accepts, generated from the live Cobra tree, so the map can't drift from what the binary dispatches. It needs no credentials, no network, and no tenant.

The default output is an indented outline headed by the version and three counts — families, commands, and executable leaves — with each command's one-line description. `--json` adds the same counts plus full command paths, aliases, and the nested tree, for scripts and for checking a docs page against a release.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i docs map [--json] [-o <file>]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--json` | Emit the tree and counts as JSON. |
| `-o`, `--output string` | Write to a file instead of stdout. `-` means stdout. |

**Example:**

```bash
c1i docs map --json | jq '{version, counts}'
```

### `docs guide`

Print an embedded, task-oriented runbook: a numbered sequence of c1i commands for a common end-to-end workflow. Run it with no argument to list the available guide names and their purposes. Guides are static content in the c1i binary, so unlike `docs search` and `docs page` they make no network call.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i docs guide [name]
```

### `docs agents`

Print a concise, agent-facing document covering the conventions that `--help` text can't: getting the tenant right, output contracts, exit codes, pagination, and when to prefer a first-class command over `c1i api`. It doesn't list every command or flag; run `c1i --help`, `c1i <group> --help`, or `c1i <group> <command> --help` for those, or `c1i docs map` for the whole tree at once.

The output opens with a YAML front-matter block (name, description, version, and required binaries) that some agent harnesses parse automatically, followed by plain Markdown. `skill` remains an alias for backward compatibility, so `c1i docs skill` still works.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `-o`, `--output string` | Write to a file instead of stdout. |

**Examples:**

```bash
# Write directly to a file for Claude Code
c1i docs agents -o .claude/skills/c1i/SKILL.md

# Write as a Cursor project rule
c1i docs agents -o .cursor/rules/c1i.md
```

For step-by-step setup instructions for Claude Code, Cursor, and other agents, see [Use c1i with AI agents](/product/cli/c1i-agent-skills).

### `docs readme` and `docs changelog`

Print the README or CHANGELOG embedded in the c1i binary, unmodified. They make no network call and need no credentials. Release archives ship only the binary, so these are how an installed c1i reads its own full reference. `docs agents` stays the concise guide for agents; the README is the full reference.

Relative links in the printed text don't resolve. A notice on stderr gives the version-pinned GitHub URL that renders the Markdown.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `-o`, `--output string` | Write to a file instead of stdout. `-` means stdout. |

<Callout icon="globe" color="#c937ae" iconType="regular">These examples use `conductor.one`. If your organization is on the EU data residency instance, substitute `c1eu.ai` in URLs, client IDs, and hostnames.</Callout>

## Auth

Log in, check which identity and credential c1i is using, and mint access tokens.

c1i reads credentials from `C1I_CLIENT_ID` and `C1I_CLIENT_SECRET`, then the OS keyring, then a `0600` file, and caches each access token it mints until it's close to expiry; see [Credential storage](/product/cli/c1i#credential-storage). `auth token` and `mcp gateway` always mint a fresh token. A cached token the server rejects (clock skew, or a revocation) is dropped and re-minted automatically, once.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i auth login`](#auth-login) | Authenticate via browser or API credentials. |
| `c1i auth logout` | Remove stored c1i credentials for the current URL from both the OS keyring and the file fallback. Environment variables (`C1I_CLIENT_ID`, `C1I_CLIENT_SECRET`) are not affected. Rejects `--dry-run` and `C1I_DRY_RUN` (exit `2`): a delete cannot be previewed. |
| `c1i auth status` | Check whether valid C1 credentials are stored and working. Reports which source (environment, keyring, or file) served the active credentials. |
| [`c1i auth whoami`](#auth-whoami) | Show the authenticated principal's identity, roles, permissions, and feature flags. |
| [`c1i auth token`](#auth-token) | Print a short-lived OAuth2 bearer token for driving raw API calls yourself. |

### `auth login`

Opens a browser for OAuth device flow authorization, or accepts credentials directly.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--client-id string` | C1 API client ID (skips browser login). |
| `--client-secret string` | C1 API client secret (skips browser login). |
| `--choose-roles` | Choose the browser-login credential's roles from a menu after approval (needs a terminal). `--choose-roles=false` skips the role question. |
| `--scoped-role stringArray` | Restrict the browser-login credential to a role, by name (such as `basic-user`) or ID (repeatable). |

The device-code user code and verification URL that `auth login` prints come from the server and are printed with control characters escaped (`\x1b`, `\u202e`), so a hostile or misconfigured token endpoint can't rewrite your terminal. `\n` and `\t` are kept.

A browser login creates a credential with all of your roles; see [Limit the credential to specific roles](/product/cli/c1i#limit-the-credential-to-specific-roles). `--scoped-role` takes a role name or a role ID from [`c1i roles list`](#roles). A name that matches no role you can scope to exits with code 2 before c1i creates your credential. An unknown role ID exits with code 4. `--choose-roles` and `--scoped-role` can't be combined, and neither works with `--client-id` and `--client-secret`. `auth login` rejects `--dry-run` (exit 2), because it can't preview creating a credential.

### `auth whoami`

Show the authenticated principal: principal ID, user ID (a service principal might carry only the principal ID), counts of roles, permissions, and tenant feature flags, and display name and email when the user lookup succeeds.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `-v`, `--verbose` | Include full roles, permissions, and features arrays. |

By default, `whoami` returns a compact summary. `--verbose` replaces that summary with the raw introspect payload, which includes the full role, permission, and feature lists but not the summary's counts, `displayName`, or `email`.

Both modes add two keys that c1i resolves itself: `tenant`, the base URL this invocation targets, and `tenantSource`, where it came from (`flag`, `env`, or `config`). They appear only after your credentials are proven against that tenant, so check them before a write:

```bash
c1i auth whoami --fields tenant
```

### `auth token`

c1i mints a new token from your stored credentials on each invocation and doesn't write it to disk.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--json` | Print a JSON object with `access_token`, `token_type`, and `expires_at` (RFC3339) instead of just the access token. |

By default only the access token is printed (newline-terminated), so it composes directly into another command:

```bash
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $(c1i auth token)" \
  https://your-tenant.conductor.one/api/v1/apps
```

The token is audience-scoped to the C1 API host.

## Roles

List and inspect IAM roles, including the role IDs that `c1i auth login --scoped-role` accepts.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i roles list` | List IAM roles. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| `c1i roles get <role-id>` | Get a single IAM role, including its permissions. |

## Users

Manage C1 users.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i users list`](#users-list) | Search and list C1 users. |
| `c1i users get <user-id>` | Get a single user by ID. |
| [`c1i users profile-types`](#users-profile-types) | Show a user's profile types across their connected apps. |
| [`c1i users set-delegation`](#users-set-delegation) | Set or clear a user's expiring delegation, as an admin. |

### `users list`

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--query string` | Fuzzy search on name or email. |
| `--email string` | Exact email match. |
| `--status string` | Filter by status: `enabled`, `disabled`, or `deleted`. |
| `--origin stringArray` | Filter by user origin: `directory`, `local`, `system`, or `all` (repeatable). A `USER_ORIGIN_*` value, as a row's `origin` prints it, is also accepted. |

With no `--origin`, the list includes only directory-origin users. It omits local-origin users, such as service principals, and system users. Pass `--origin all` to list every user. Rows carry `origin` when the server reports one.

### `users profile-types`

Show the profile types associated with a user across their connected apps, as `{"profileTypes": [...]}`. Each profile type carries `id`, `name`, `slug`, `description`, `priority`, `displayToUser`, and `iconUrl` as the API returns them. An unknown user ID also returned an empty list at exit `0`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i users profile-types <user-id>
```

### `users set-delegation`

Designate a delegate for a user, optionally for a time window. `--delegate-user-id` sets the delegate; `--clear` removes the delegation, which c1i sends as an empty delegate ID, the form the API describes as removing it.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i users set-delegation <user-id> --delegate-user-id <user-id> \
  [--start-at <RFC3339>] [--expire-at <RFC3339>]
c1i users set-delegation <user-id> --clear
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--delegate-user-id string` | User who acts as delegate. Required unless `--clear`. |
| `--start-at string` | RFC3339 time the delegation starts. Omit for no start. |
| `--expire-at string` | RFC3339 time the delegation expires. Omit for no expiry. |
| `--clear` | Remove the delegation. Mutually exclusive with the other flags. |

Observed against a tenant: an unknown delegate answered `404` (exit `4`), an expiry before the start answered `400` "end date must be after start date for temporary user delegation" (exit `2`), and a start or expiry in the past and a self-delegation were accepted at exit `0`. Clearing returned `{"item": null}`; a second clear, or a clear on a user who never had a delegation, answered `404` "provided delegated user id is empty" (exit `4`). Immediately after a set, the clear's lookup missed the new binding for a few seconds in one run. `users get` showed `delegatedUserId` empty while the start was still in the future, so the printed `.item` is the confirmation.

The command prints the binding unwrapped from the response's `.item`, or the response as is (`{"item": null}`) when clearing.

## Apps

Create, search, inspect, update, and delete applications, manage their owners, and manage what hangs off an app: its resource types and resources, entitlement routing rules, and usage controls. Apps a connector discovered can be promoted to managed apps.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i apps list`](#apps-list) | List applications. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| [`c1i apps search`](#apps-search) | Search applications by name, description, id, policy, or directory. |
| `c1i apps get <app-id>` | Get a single app by ID. |
| [`c1i apps create`](#apps-create) | Create a new app. |
| [`c1i apps update`](#apps-update) | Update an app's name, description, policies, or identity matching. |
| `c1i apps delete <app-id>` | Soft-delete an app by ID. |
| [`c1i apps owners`](#apps-owners) | List an app's owners, or the entitlements that own it. |
| [`c1i apps add-owner`](#apps-add-owner) | Add one owner to an app. |
| [`c1i apps remove-owner`](#apps-remove-owner) | Remove one owner from an app. |
| [`c1i apps set-owners`](#apps-set-owners) | Set the owners of an app, replacing the full owner list. |
| [`c1i apps managed-state`](#apps-managed-state) | Inspect and promote apps a connector discovered. |
| [`c1i apps resource-types`](#apps-resource-types) | List, search, get, create, rename, and delete an app's resource types. |
| [`c1i apps resources`](#apps-resources) | List, search, get, create, update, and delete an app's resources, and manage their owners. |
| [`c1i apps routing-rules`](#apps-routing-rules) | Manage an app's entitlement routing rules: the CEL rules that decide a matching entitlement's request settings. |
| [`c1i apps usage-controls get`](#apps-usage-controls-get) | Show whether C1 notifies or revokes users who have access but no usage. |

`user_count` on an app row is the platform's stored count, not a live tally: on some apps it differs from the number of [`accounts list`](#accounts-list) rows. To size a change, count those rows, not `user_count`.

`apps list` takes no filters at all, only pagination. To narrow by name, id, policy, or directory, use [`apps search`](#apps-search), which emits the same rows.

### `apps list`

List applications. A soft-deleted app drops out of the list while [`apps get`](#apps) still returns it with `deletedAt` set. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

The command takes no query filter. See [`apps search`](#apps-search) for filtering.

### `apps search`

Search applications server-side, or narrow a list to specific apps. Rows are the same shape as `apps list`. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits); `--page-size` defaults to 25.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps search [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--query string` | Full-text search on display name and description. Not a substring match: the server matches tokens, stems, and 3-character n-grams, and 1–2 character terms behave inconsistently. |
| `--display-name string` | Case-insensitive server match. The value is an ILIKE pattern, so `%` and `_` are wildcards. |
| `--app-id stringArray` | Restrict to this app ID (repeatable). |
| `--exclude-app-id stringArray` | App ID to exclude from results (repeatable). |
| `--policy-id stringArray` | Only apps that use any of the given policy IDs (repeatable). Each ID is looked up first: an unknown one exits `4` and a deleted one `2`, before the search is sent. |
| `--only-directories` | Set the server's `onlyDirectories` filter. |

An `--app-id` the tenant doesn't have matches nothing: zero rows and exit `0`. A `.` or `..` ID is refused locally (exit `2`); another malformed ID is rejected by the server (exit `2`).

### `apps create`

Create a new app — a plain, unmanaged container you can register MCP servers under. Only `--display-name` is required. C1 assigns the caller as an owner of the new app; manage owners afterward with `add-owner`, `remove-owner`, or `set-owners`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps create --display-name <name> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--display-name string` | Display name for the new app. Can't be blank. (Required.) |
| `--description string` | Description for the new app. |
| `--grant-policy-id string` | Grant policy ID to bind; must exist and be a grant policy. Omit for the server's default. |
| `--revoke-policy-id string` | Revoke policy ID to bind; must exist and be a revoke policy. Omit for the server's default. |
| `--certify-policy-id string` | Certify policy ID to bind; must exist and be a certify policy. |
| `--identity-matching string` | How app users are matched to C1 users: `strict`, `display-name`, `custom`, or the full `APP_USER_IDENTITY_MATCHING_*` name of one. |

Before sending, c1i checks each policy ID. An ID that doesn't exist exits with code 4; one that is deleted or of the wrong type exits with code 2. Nothing is sent. `--dry-run` runs the same check. An empty policy ID is a usage error.

The created app is returned as formatted JSON, unwrapped like `apps get`, so the app's ID is `.id`.

**Example:**

```bash
APP_ID=$(c1i apps create --display-name "Google Workspace" | jq -er .id)
```

### `apps update`

Update an app's display name, description, policies, or identity matching. c1i builds the update mask from the flags you pass: an omitted flag is left alone, and an explicit empty value is a change.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps update <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--display-name string` | New display name. Can't be blank. |
| `--description string` | New description. Pass an empty value to clear it. |
| `--grant-policy-id string` | Grant policy ID to bind; must exist and be a grant policy. |
| `--revoke-policy-id string` | Revoke policy ID to bind; must exist and be a revoke policy. |
| `--certify-policy-id string` | Certify policy ID to bind; must exist and be a certify policy. |
| `--unbind-policy stringArray` | Unbind a policy: `grant`, `revoke`, or `certify` (repeatable). |
| `--identity-matching string` | How app users are matched to C1 users: `strict`, `display-name`, `custom`, or the full `APP_USER_IDENTITY_MATCHING_*` name of one. |
| `--app-user-mapper-file string` | JSON object that replaces the app's whole user mapper (file, or `-` for stdin). |

c1i reads the app before sending, `--dry-run` included: a malformed ID is a usage error (exit `2`), an unknown app exits `4`, and a soft-deleted one `2`.

Each policy ID is checked before anything is sent, as for `apps create`. An empty policy ID is a usage error rather than an unbind, so pass `--unbind-policy` to remove a policy. You can't set and unbind the same kind in one call.

`--app-user-mapper-file` takes an object such as `{"mappingCases":[{"userKeyCel":"subject.email","appUserKeyCel":"account.email"}]}`. `userKeyCel` runs against the C1 user as `subject`, and `appUserKeyCel` runs against the app user as `account`. `mappingCases` must be non-empty, and the API offers no way to remove a mapper.

The updated app is returned as formatted JSON, unwrapped like `apps get`.

### `apps delete`

Soft-delete an app: it is marked deleted and drops out of [`apps list`](#apps-list), but [`apps get`](#apps) still returns it with `deletedAt` set. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success. An app's entitlements stay live, so delete them with [`entitlements delete`](#entitlements-delete) first.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps delete <app-id>
```

### `apps owners`

List the owners of an app, the read that reflects what `set-owners`, `add-owner`, and `remove-owner` write. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps owners <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--entitlements` | List the entitlements that own the app instead of its users, from the v2 owner route (role `primary`). Rows carry the owning entitlement's `id`, `app_id`, `display_name`, `slug`, `alias`, `role_slug`, `added_at`, and `deleted_at`. |

Owner changes are applied asynchronously and can take several minutes to appear. Most apps already have at least one owner, because `apps create` assigns its caller as an owner.

A well-formed app ID that doesn't exist returns zero rows and exits `0`, not `4`, so an empty result means either no owners or a wrong ID; `apps add-owner` and `apps remove-owner` on the same ID exit `4` and can tell you which. With `--entitlements`, an unknown ID exits `4`.

Don't read ownership from the `appOwners` field of `apps get`: it read empty on every app checked, including apps this command lists owners for.

### `apps add-owner`

Add one owner to an app without changing the rest of the owner list. Unlike `set-owners`, it can't drop an owner that someone else added in the meantime.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps add-owner <app-id> (--user-id <user-id> | --owner-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> [--owner-entitlement-app-id <app-id>])
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--user-id string` | C1 user ID to add as owner. One per call, at most once; use `set-owners` for several. |
| `--owner-entitlement-id string` | Entitlement whose grantees own the app. Exactly one of this and `--user-id` is required. |
| `--owner-entitlement-app-id string` | App of `--owner-entitlement-id` when it differs from the app being owned (default: the owned app). |

An unknown app exits `4` and a deleted one `2`. With `--user-id`, c1i looks the user up before sending, including under `--dry-run`: an unknown ID exits `4` and nothing is sent, and the lookup needs user-read access (exit `3` without it). Adding an entitlement that is already an owner exits `0`. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

Owner changes are applied asynchronously; verify with `c1i apps owners <app-id>`.

With `--owner-entitlement-id` the write goes through the v2 owner route under the role `primary` (the only role the API supports). That form takes no `--wait` (exit `2`): the owner is in place when the command returns.

### `apps remove-owner`

Remove one owner from an app without changing the rest of the owner list. The creator that `apps create` assigned can remain an owner, so check `c1i apps owners <app-id>` first if that matters.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps remove-owner <app-id> (--user-id <user-id> | --owner-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> [--owner-entitlement-app-id <app-id>])
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--user-id string` | C1 user ID to remove as owner. One per call, at most once; use `set-owners` for several. |
| `--owner-entitlement-id string` | Entitlement whose grantees own the app. Exactly one of this and `--user-id` is required. |
| `--owner-entitlement-app-id string` | App of `--owner-entitlement-id` when it differs from the app being owned (default: the owned app). |

An unknown app exits `4` and a deleted one `2`. c1i doesn't look the `--user-id` user up first, so the server decides and a `--dry-run` preview can pass an ID the real run refuses. Removing an entitlement owner a second time exits `0`; an unknown entitlement exits `4`. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

Owner changes are applied asynchronously; verify with `c1i apps owners <app-id>`.

### `apps set-owners`

Replace an app's complete owner list with exactly the IDs you pass, so an owner added between your read and your write is silently removed — including the creator `apps create` auto-assigned.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps set-owners <app-id> (--user-id <user-id> | --owner-entitlement-id <entitlement-id>) [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--user-id stringArray` | C1 user ID to set as owner (repeatable; replaces the user owners). |
| `--owner-entitlement-id stringArray` | Entitlement whose grantees own the app (repeatable; replaces both the user and the entitlement owners). |
| `--owner-entitlement-app-id string` | App of the `--owner-entitlement-id` values when it differs from the app being owned (default: the owned app). |
| `--no-user-owners` | With `--owner-entitlement-id` and no `--user-id`, remove every user owner. |
| `--wait` | Block and poll until the owner set equals the requested owners, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. A timeout exits with code 1, though the write was accepted. |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Max time to wait with `--wait` (default `4m0s`). |

At least one `--user-id` or `--owner-entitlement-id` is required. c1i reads the app first, `--dry-run` included: an unknown app exits `4`, a soft-deleted one `2`, and a preview therefore needs credentials.

Before it sends the request, c1i looks up each distinct `--user-id`. An unknown user exits with code 4, naming the ID, and nothing is sent, because the API would accept the unknown ID and store it. The lookup needs user-read access, so a credential without it exits with code 3. `--dry-run` runs the same lookups. c1i sends a repeated `--user-id` once.

With any `--owner-entitlement-id`, the set goes through the v2 owner route, which replaces the entitlement owners and is meant to replace the user owners with the `--user-id` values. Repeat `--user-id` for each user owner to keep, or pass `--no-user-owners` to remove them all; with neither, c1i refuses (exit `2`) and sends nothing. `--no-user-owners` needs `--owner-entitlement-id` and excludes `--user-id`. Without `--owner-entitlement-id`, the command replaces user owners only and leaves entitlement owners in place.

Owner changes are applied asynchronously, so the command returns once the request is accepted and new owners can take several minutes to appear. `--wait` polls the v1 owner ID list — and, in the entitlement form, the v2 entitlement owner list too — until both equal the request, including removals. `--wait` progress goes to stderr, and the command prints the API's JSON response on stdout.

**Example:**

```bash
c1i apps set-owners app_abc123 --user-id user_1 --user-id user_2 --wait
```

### `apps managed-state`

Inspect and promote the apps a connector discovered, such as apps in an identity provider's catalog. A discovered app is a resource of the connector app's app-trait resource type. Until it is promoted, its state is `unmanaged`; `promote` creates a managed app and returns `managed` with that app's id.

Find the resource type first with [`apps resource-types list`](#apps-resource-types). Every subcommand takes `--app-id` (the connector app) and `--resource-type-id`.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i apps managed-state list` | List discovered apps and whether they are managed. Rows carry `resource_id`, `display_name`, `state`, and `managed_app_id` (null while unmanaged). [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| `c1i apps managed-state get <resource-id>` | Get one discovered app's managed state, unwrapped: the state is under `state` (`{"unmanaged":{}}` or `{"managed":{"appId":"<managed-app-id>"}}`), and the top-level `appId` is the connector app. |
| `c1i apps managed-state promote <resource-id>` | Create a managed app for the discovered app and print the binding. Read the new app's ID with `.state.managed.appId`. |

A well-formed but unknown app or resource type lists zero rows and exits `0`; `get` and `promote` on the same IDs exit `4`.

`promote` accepts repeatable `--user-id` to set the new app's owners; omit it to inherit the connector app's owners. Each user is looked up first, so an unknown one exits `4`, `--dry-run` included.

Promoting an already-managed binding exits `0` and returns the existing app ID, creating no second app. Promoting does not check for an existing app with the same name, so check [`apps list`](#apps-list) first if a duplicate name matters. After deleting the managed app, the binding's `deletedAt` can lag by a few seconds; wait until `managed-state get` shows it before promoting again, or you can create a second app. The binding later reappears unmanaged under the same `resource_id`, after which a promote creates a new app.

### `apps resource-types`

Manage an app's resource types: the groups a resource belongs to and the parent `--resource-type-id` that the resource and discovered-app commands take. Each type belongs to the app given by `--app-id`.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i apps resource-types list --app-id <app-id>` | List an app's resource types. A connector app's discovered apps are in its type named `app`. Rows gain `app_id` in v0.12.0 and otherwise match `search`. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| `c1i apps resource-types search` | Search resource types across the tenant, or across the apps named by `--app-id`. |
| `c1i apps resource-types get <resource-type-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Get one resource type, unwrapped. A deleted type is still returned, with `deletedAt` set. |
| `c1i apps resource-types create --app-id <app-id> --display-name <name> --resource-type <kind>` | Create a manually managed resource type. |
| `c1i apps resource-types update <resource-type-id> --app-id <app-id> --display-name <name>` | Rename a manually managed resource type. |
| `c1i apps resource-types delete <resource-type-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Soft-delete a manually managed resource type. |

`create --resource-type` is case-insensitive and takes the API spec's kinds: `role`, `group`, `license`, `project`, `catalog`, `custom`, `vault`, `profile-type`, `session-policy`, or `claw-agent`. It has no default. Only `CUSTOM` can repeat on one app: a second type of any other kind fails with a 500 (exit `6`, though retrying never helps) saying `app resource type already exists`. A `--display-name` outside the server's length range is a 400 (exit `2`) whose message states the range.

`search` filters with `--query` (fuzzy match on the display name), `--display-name` (case-insensitive match), and repeatable `--app-id`, `--resource-type-id`, `--exclude-resource-type-id`, `--trait-id`, and `--exclude-trait-id`. Several values of one flag match any of them; an ID that matches nothing returns zero rows and exit `0`. One resource type ID can appear on several apps (a row per app), so key rows on `app_id` and `id`.

`update` renames only: the API's other writable field, `traitIds`, was answered `200` and left unchanged, so there is no flag for it.

Deleting a type is soft: `get` still returns it with `deletedAt` set and `list` stops listing it; a repeat delete exits `0` and an unknown type exits `4`. The API spec says a type delete also deletes its resources, but a resource of a deleted type was still live ten minutes later — delete the resources first with [`apps resources delete`](#apps-resources) if they must go. Creating a resource under a deleted type fails with a 500 (exit `6`).

### `apps resources`

Manage an app's resources: the objects an entitlement points at, plus their owners. Every per-resource subcommand takes `--app-id` and `--resource-type-id`, the resource's parent scope.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i apps resources list` | List the resources of one resource type. Rows match `search` rows; `grant_count` is a number. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| `c1i apps resources search` | Search resources across the tenant, or across the apps named by `--app-id`. |
| `c1i apps resources get <resource-id>` | Get one resource, unwrapped. A deleted resource is still returned, with `deletedAt` set. |
| `c1i apps resources create` | Create a manually managed resource. |
| `c1i apps resources update <resource-id>` | Update a resource's name or descriptions. |
| `c1i apps resources delete <resource-id>` | Soft-delete a manually managed resource. |
| `c1i apps resources owners <resource-id>` | List a resource's owners, or the entitlements that own it. |
| `c1i apps resources add-owner <resource-id>` | Add one owner to a resource. |
| `c1i apps resources remove-owner <resource-id>` | Remove one owner from a resource. |
| `c1i apps resources set-owners <resource-id>` | Set the owners of a resource, replacing the full owner list. |

`list` returns zero rows and exit `0` for a well-formed but unknown resource type. `search` filters with `--query` (fuzzy match on the name), repeatable `--app-id`, `--resource-type-id`, `--resource-id`, `--exclude-resource-id`, `--trait-id`, `--exclude-trait-id`, `--owner-user-id`, and `--unowned-only`. `--owner-user-id` keeps resources owned by any of the given users; `--unowned-only` keeps those with no owner. An ID that matches nothing returns zero rows and exit `0`. One resource ID can appear on several apps (a row per app), so key rows on `app_id`, `resource_type_id`, and `id`.

`create` takes `--display-name` (plus optional `--description` and repeatable `--owner-id`), or `--body-file` for a whole create request, which is the only way to set annotations or `matchBatonId`; the body must not set `appId` or `appResourceTypeId` (exit `2`). The resource type is looked up first: a type that doesn't exist exits `4` and a deleted one `2`. Each `--owner-id` is looked up first too, so an unknown user exits `4`. A `--display-name` outside the server's length range is a 400 (exit `2`) stating the range.

`update` builds the update mask from the flags you pass: `--display-name`, `--description`, and `--custom-description` (`""` clears the custom description). The server ignores an empty name or description, so a blank one exits `2` here, nothing sent. For other fields (annotations, for one) pass an `AppResource` object with `--body-file` plus an explicit `--update-mask` (both required together and mutually exclusive with the flags above); mask paths are camelCase and comma-separated, and annotations sent that way are merged into the existing ones. An unknown app, resource type, or resource exits `4`, and a deleted app `2`.

`delete` is soft: `get` still returns the resource with `deletedAt` set, while `list` and `search` stop returning it (they can trail the delete by a few seconds). A repeat delete exits `0`; an unknown app, resource type, or resource exits `4`.

`owners` returns zero rows and exit `0` for an unknown resource ID or a wrong `--app-id`; it ignores `--resource-type-id` because the server resolves the resource on its own, so use `resources get` with the same IDs to validate them (it exits `4`). Rows match `apps owners` rows. The owners response also carries `immutableUserIds` — owners the API says cannot be removed — which these rows leave out; read it with `c1i api`. `--entitlements` lists the entitlements that own the resource instead, from the v2 owner route, and there an unknown ID exits `4`.

`add-owner`, `remove-owner`, and `set-owners` take exactly one of `--user-id` or `--owner-entitlement-id` (`set-owners` accepts several of either), plus `--owner-entitlement-app-id` when the entitlement's app differs from the owned resource's. `add-owner` and `set-owners` take `--wait` / `--wait-timeout`; `remove-owner` does not. The API answers `set-owners` on an unknown resource with `200 {}` and stores the owners anyway, so c1i looks the resource up first and exits `4`. An unknown resource, resource type, or app exits `4`; a deleted app exits `2`. Adding an owner that is already one, or removing one that is not, exits `0`.

### `apps routing-rules`

Manage an app's entitlement routing rules (the API's entitlement configuration rules). Rules are evaluated in priority order — 1 first — and the first enabled rule whose CEL `--condition` matches supplies the entitlement's request settings: the request policy, emergency grant policy and enablement, maximum grant duration, and request schema. An enabled rule routes real access requests, so create a rule disabled (the default) to stage it.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i apps routing-rules list --app-id <app-id>` | List an app's rules in evaluation order. Rows carry `id`, `app_id`, `display_name`, `description`, `priority`, `enabled`, `condition` (null matches every target no earlier rule matched), the settings flattened (`request_policy_id`, `emergency_grant_policy_id`, `emergency_grant_enabled`, `max_grant_duration`, `request_schema_id`), and timestamps. Takes no paging: the endpoint returns the whole set. |
| `c1i apps routing-rules get <rule-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Get one rule, unwrapped. A deleted rule is still returned, with `deletedAt` set. |
| [`c1i apps routing-rules create`](#apps-routing-rules) | Create a rule on an app. |
| `c1i apps routing-rules update <rule-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Change a rule's fields. |
| `c1i apps routing-rules delete <rule-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Soft-delete a rule; the rest keep their order, renumbered 1..N. A repeat delete exits `4`. |
| `c1i apps routing-rules reorder --app-id <app-id> --rule-id <id>` | Set the evaluation order: pass every active rule's ID once, in order, and they get priorities 1..N. |

`list` returns zero rows and exit `0` for a well-formed but unknown app; `get`, `create`, `update`, and `delete` exit `4` for an unknown app or rule. `create`, `update`, and `reorder` read the app first, `--dry-run` included: a deleted app exits `2`. `update` also refuses a deleted rule (`2`), while `get` reports a deleted app as unknown (`4`).

`create` and `update` set the same settings. The server checks referenced IDs: an unknown policy or request schema exits `4`, and a policy that is not a grant policy exits `2` (`request_policy_id must reference a grant policy`). `--emergency-grant-enabled` needs `--emergency-grant-policy-id`. An invalid `--condition` is a 400 (exit `2`) quoting the parse error.

`create --priority` is a 1-indexed insertion slot: the rule goes there and later rules move down one; a slot past the end, or omitting the flag, appends. The server accepts `0`–`1000`. An app held a limited, measured maximum of 10 rules; past it the server answers a 400 (exit `2`).

`reorder` is refused (400, exit `2`) unless `--rule-id` names every active rule exactly once; nothing is reordered. It prints `{"list": [...]}`: every rule in its new order.

`update` has no mask to build: the API replaces the whole rule and clears any writable field the request leaves out, so c1i reads the rule and re-sends every writable field with your flags merged in. An edit made between that read and the write is overwritten. Priority is server-assigned on update and preserved; change the order with `reorder`. `--description ""` and `--condition ""` clear those fields, but a policy or schema ID, and `--max-grant-duration`, can be changed and not unset here.

`create` takes:

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps routing-rules create --app-id <app-id> --display-name <name> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | ID of the app to create the rule on. (Required.) |
| `--display-name string` | Display name of the new rule. (Required.) |
| `--description string` | Description of the new rule. |
| `--condition string` | CEL condition a target must match. Omit to match every target no earlier rule matched. |
| `--enabled` | Create the rule enabled, so it routes real requests. Disabled unless given. |
| `--priority int` | 1-indexed insertion slot. Omit to append last. |
| `--request-policy-id string` | Grant policy a matching request uses. |
| `--emergency-grant-policy-id string` | Grant policy for emergency access requests. |
| `--emergency-grant-enabled` | Allow emergency access requests; needs `--emergency-grant-policy-id`. |
| `--max-grant-duration string` | Longest grant a matching request may ask for, as a protobuf duration such as `3600s` or a Go duration such as `8h`. |
| `--request-schema-id string` | Request schema (form) a matching request fills in. |

**Example:**

```bash
c1i apps routing-rules create --app-id <app-id> --display-name "Engineering" \
  --condition 'entitlement.displayName == "Admin"' --request-policy-id <policy-id>
```

### `apps usage-controls get`

Show an app's usage controls: whether C1 notifies, and whether it revokes, users who hold access to the app but have not used it within a number of days.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i apps usage-controls get <app-id>
```

Output is unwrapped: `appId`, `notify`, `notifyAfterDays`, `revoke`, and `revokeAfterDays` at the top level, beside `hasUsageData` (`false` when the app's access entitlement has no usage data). An app whose controls were never set prints only `hasUsageData`.

The usage-controls endpoint answers an unknown app as it does an app with no controls, so c1i reads the app first: an unknown app exits `4` and a deleted one `2`. There is no `update` command.

## Accounts

Manage application accounts (app users): the accounts C1 syncs from an app, the identity user each one maps to, and the account's profile. Every command takes `--app-id`, the application ID.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i accounts list`](#accounts-list) | Search and list an application's accounts. |
| [`c1i accounts get`](#accounts-get) | Get a single account by ID. |
| [`c1i accounts set-owner`](#accounts-set-owner) | Set the identity user that owns an account. |

### `accounts list`

Search and list accounts for an application. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i accounts list --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--status string` | Filter by status: `enabled`, `disabled`, `deleted`, or any `STATUS_*` value. |
| `--type string` | Filter by type: `user`, `service-account`, `system-account`, or any `APP_USER_TYPE_*` value. The two multi-word values use hyphens. |
| `--unmapped-only` | Only show accounts with no linked identity user. |
| `--query string` | Fuzzy search on display name. |

Rows carry `profile` in v0.12.0: the app user's profile as a JSON object, or `null` when absent. `--fields profile.<key>` reaches one attribute without the round trip through [`accounts get`](#accounts-get).

### `accounts get`

Get a single application account by ID.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i accounts get <app-user-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

There is no public get-by-ID route for app users. This runs the same app-user search `accounts list` does, filtered to the one ID under `--app-id`, and prints the app user unwrapped like the other reads — including `profile`, which `accounts list` rows carry too. An ID that belongs to a different app is not found here on purpose: zero matches exits `4`, and more than one match for the same ID is a C1 failure (exit `6`).

### `accounts set-owner`

Set the C1 identity user that owns an app account.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i accounts set-owner <app-user-id> --app-id <app-id> --user-id <user-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--user-id string` | C1 user ID to set as owner. (Required; at most once.) |

The updated app user is returned as formatted JSON, unwrapped like the reads: the account's ID is `.id` and its owner is `.identityUserId`.

c1i looks the `--user-id` user up before sending, `--dry-run` included: an unknown ID exits `4` and nothing is sent, and the lookup needs user-read access (exit `3` without it).

## Attribute types

List the user fields an attribute mapping can write to, such as Department, Job Title, or Manager Email. Type IDs are tenant-specific, so look a type up by display name; `attribute-mappings create --type` does the same.

### `attribute-types list`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attribute-types list [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--limit int` | Cap the number of rows. The endpoint returns the whole set and takes no page-size or page-token argument. |

Rows include `id`, `display_name`, and `config_kind`: `app` (a value read from the app itself, such as Manager Email), `app_user_profile` (read from one profile key of the app's users), `custom_app_user_profile` (Custom: the mapping also names the custom user attribute), or `cel_expression` (create refuses it; use `--body-file`). `config_kind` is `null` when none is set.

A mapping's `config_kind` decides which key flags it accepts. A `cel_expression` type cannot be created with the convenience flags; use `--body-file`.

## Attribute mappings

Map an app's synced profile attributes onto C1 user fields. A mapping copies one value from an app (a profile key, or a value the app itself reports such as manager email) into a structured C1 user field, and its `display_name` is set by the server, not by a flag. Values are copied verbatim, so `Full-Time` stays `Full-Time` and a CEL comparison against a lowercase literal will not match. Evaluation is asynchronous and no response signals completion: re-read the user.

Precedence when two apps map the same attribute type was not tested — do not add a mapping for a type another app already maps on users that overlap.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i attribute-mappings list`](#attribute-mappings-list) | List mappings, optionally only one app's. |
| [`c1i attribute-mappings get`](#attribute-mappings-get) | Get one mapping. |
| [`c1i attribute-mappings create`](#attribute-mappings-create) | Create a mapping. |
| [`c1i attribute-mappings update`](#attribute-mappings-update) | Change the profile key or fallbacks. |
| [`c1i attribute-mappings delete`](#attribute-mappings-delete) | Delete a mapping. |

### `attribute-mappings list`

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attribute-mappings list [--app-id <app-id>] [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Only list mappings of this app. The endpoint has no app filter, so c1i applies it after each page is read and still fetches every page. |

Rows carry `id`, `app_id`, `key`, `display_name`, `attribute_type_id`, `attribute_type_name`, `config_kind` (see [attribute types](#attribute-types)), `priority`, `count`, and `evaluation_error_count`. `key` is `null` for an app-level type such as Manager Email.

### `attribute-mappings get`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attribute-mappings get <mapping-id>
```

The mapping is unwrapped: its ID is `.id`, the type's config is `.attributeType`, and `appPath` and `fallbacksPath` stay as siblings.

### `attribute-mappings create`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attribute-mappings create --app-id <app-id> --type <type> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | App whose users the mapping reads. Required unless `--body-file`. |
| `--type string` | Attribute type display name or ID. Required unless `--body-file`. |
| `--key string` | Profile key to read. Required for `app_user_profile` and `custom_app_user_profile`; refused for an `app` type. |
| `--custom-key string` | Custom user attribute to write. Required for `custom_app_user_profile`. |
| `--fallbacks-file string` | JSON array of fallback sources (file, or `-` for stdin). |
| `--profile-type-id stringArray` | Profile type ID to bind (repeatable). |
| `--body-file string` | Full create request JSON, sent verbatim and with nothing looked up. Mutually exclusive with the other flags. |

c1i looks the type up before sending, including under `--dry-run`: an unknown type exits `4`, and a name shared by several types exits `2` with their IDs. The request body then follows the type's config kind, so you never pick it; `--key` on an `app` type exits `2` rather than letting the server answer a wrong key with a 500.

`--fallbacks-file` is sent as given, for example `[{"appUserProfile":{"appId":"<app-id>","appUserProfileAttributeKey":"alt"}}]`. `--profile-type-id` binds profile types, but the server refuses it on a built-in type (`builtin attributes cannot be bound to profile types`).

A second create for the same app and type is not refused; it makes a second mapping with its own ID, so list first in a script. A real run with an unknown app ID exits `4`; `--dry-run` looks up the type but not the app, so it can preview a request the server would refuse.

The created mapping is returned as formatted JSON, unwrapped like `get`.

**Example:**

```bash
c1i attribute-mappings create --app-id <app-id> --type "Department" --key department
c1i attribute-mappings create --app-id <app-id> --type "Manager Email"
```

### `attribute-mappings update`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attribute-mappings update <mapping-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--key string` | New profile key to read. |
| `--custom-key string` | New custom user attribute; `custom_app_user_profile` mappings only. |
| `--fallbacks-file string` | JSON array replacing the fallbacks; `[]` clears them. |

The API's `PUT` replaces the mapping: it requires the whole `attributeType`, clears fallbacks and profile types that are left out, and ignores `updateMask`. So c1i reads the current mapping and re-sends its attribute type, fallbacks, and profile types with only these flags changed, and still sends the mask it builds (`attributeType, fallbacks`) as the API documents. `--dry-run` does the read too. The read and write are not atomic; an edit made between them is overwritten. The type and app cannot be changed, and the type's config kind decides which key flags apply (`--key` on an app-level type exits `2`).

A changed `--key` reached the mapped users within a minute in the run measured.

### `attribute-mappings delete`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attribute-mappings delete <mapping-id>
```

After the delete, `get` exits `4` and an unknown ID exits `4`, but deleting the same ID again exits `0`. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

## Attributes

List, search, create, and delete the tenant's attribute values: the entries under an attribute type, such as the Compliance Framework values (`SOC2`, `HIPAA`) and Risk Level values (`Low`, `High`). This is a separate resource from [attribute types](#attribute-types) and [attribute mappings](#attribute-mappings), which describe the user fields a mapping writes to.

A value has no update; delete it and create another. The API's per-type routes (`/api/v1/attributes/compliance_frameworks`, `/api/v1/attributes/risk_levels`, and so on) are the same operations restricted to one type, so they have no commands here; use [`c1i api`](#api) for them.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i attributes types`](#attributes-types) | List the attribute types that have values. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| [`c1i attributes list`](#attributes-list) | List the values of one attribute type. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| [`c1i attributes search`](#attributes-search) | Search values by type, id, value, or text. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). |
| [`c1i attributes get`](#attributes-get-and-attributes-delete) | Get one value by ID. A soft-deleted value is still returned, with `deleted_at` set. |
| [`c1i attributes create`](#attributes-create) | Create a value under a type. |
| [`c1i attributes delete`](#attributes-get-and-attributes-delete) | Soft-delete a value. |

### `attributes types`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attributes types [flags]
```

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). Rows are `id` and `name`.

### `attributes list`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attributes list --type <type> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--type string` | Attribute type name or id. (Required.) |

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). `--type` takes a type's name or id and is looked up first, so an unknown type exits `4` before the list is sent. Rows are `id`, `type_id`, `value`, `created_at`, `updated_at`, and `deleted_at` (`null` for a live value).

```bash
c1i attributes list --type "Compliance Framework"
```

### `attributes search`

Search values across types.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attributes search [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--type stringArray` | Only values of this attribute type, by name or id (repeatable). |
| `--id stringArray` | Only this value id (repeatable). |
| `--exclude-id stringArray` | Leave out this value id (repeatable). |
| `--value string` | Case-insensitive match on the value. |
| `--query string` | Fuzzy search on the value. |

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits). Each `--type` is looked up first, so an unknown one exits `4` (the server answers an unknown type id with an empty list). Rows are the same shape `attributes list` emits.

### `attributes get` and `attributes delete`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attributes get <value-id>
c1i attributes delete <value-id>
```

`get` prints the value as JSON. `delete` is a soft delete: the value leaves `attributes list` and `attributes search`, while `attributes get` still returns it with `deleted_at` set. Deleting it again exits `0`; an unknown id exits `4` on either command. `delete` prints `{}`, so exit `0` is the success signal.

### `attributes create`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i attributes create --type <type> --value <value>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--type string` | Attribute type name or id. (Required.) |
| `--value string` | The value to create. (Required.) |

`--type` is looked up first, including under `--dry-run`: an unknown type exits `4`, because the server would otherwise create a value under it anyway.

The server treats a repeated value, including one that differs only in case, as a new value with its own id. It rejects an empty value and one over 512 bytes. `create` prints the new value unwrapped like `attributes get`, so its id is `.id`.

```bash
c1i attributes create --type "Risk Level" --value High
```

## Entitlements

Find, inspect, create, update, and delete app entitlements; list their grants; manage their owners; and configure what hangs off an entitlement: its membership automation, its account-provisioning policy, and entitlement-to-entitlement proxy bindings.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i entitlements list`](#entitlements-list) | Search and list application entitlements. |
| [`c1i entitlements get`](#entitlements-get) | Get a single app entitlement by ID. |
| [`c1i entitlements create`](#entitlements-create) | Create an app entitlement, with its resource type and resource. |
| [`c1i entitlements update`](#entitlements-update) | Update an entitlement's name, description, policies, or maximum grant duration. |
| [`c1i entitlements delete`](#entitlements-delete) | Soft-delete an app entitlement. |
| [`c1i entitlements grants`](#entitlements-grants) | List an entitlement's grants with their discovered and expiry times. |
| [`c1i entitlements owners`](#entitlements-owners) | List an entitlement's owners, or the entitlements that own it. |
| [`c1i entitlements add-owner`](#entitlements-add-owner) | Add one owner to an entitlement. |
| [`c1i entitlements remove-owner`](#entitlements-remove-owner) | Remove one owner from an entitlement. |
| [`c1i entitlements set-owners`](#entitlements-set-owners) | Set the owners of an entitlement, replacing the full owner list. |
| [`c1i entitlements automation`](#entitlements-automation) | Manage an entitlement's membership automation and its exclusion list. |
| [`c1i entitlements account-provisioning`](#entitlements-account-provisioning) | Show or set an entitlement's connector account-provisioning policy. |
| [`c1i entitlements proxy-bindings`](#entitlements-proxy-bindings) | Manage directional entitlement proxy bindings. |

An entitlement ID is only unique within an app, so always key on the app ID and the entitlement ID together. Some built-in entitlements, such as the base `Access` entitlement, use the same ID in every app that has one; that is by design, not data corruption.

### `entitlements list`

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Filter by application ID. |
| `--query string` | Search entitlement display names. |

### `entitlements get`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements get <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |

### `entitlements create`

Create an app entitlement, plus the app resource type and resource it points at, as up to three requests in order. Pass `--resource-type-id` to reuse an existing resource type, and `--resource-id` to reuse an existing resource. Outputs the created entitlement as formatted JSON, unwrapped like `entitlements get`, so its ID is `.id`. The output echoes `appResourceTypeId` and `appResourceId` and expands both objects.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements create --app-id <app-id> --display-name <name> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID to create the entitlement on. (Required.) |
| `--display-name string` | Display name for the new entitlement; also names the resource type and resource that c1i creates. Can't be blank. (Required.) |
| `--description string` | Description for the new entitlement. |
| `--alias string` | Alias for the new entitlement; exact-match queryable. |
| `--slug string` | Slug for the new entitlement, such as `member`. |
| `--duration-grant string` | Maximum grant duration, as a whole-number Go duration such as `8h` or a protobuf duration such as `3600s`; c1i sends it in seconds. Omit for standing access. A sign, decimal, or leading zero (`-1h`, `1.5h`, `01h`) exits with code 2. |
| `--owner-id stringArray` | C1 user ID to own the new entitlement (repeatable). |
| `--grant-policy-id string` | Grant policy ID to bind; must exist and be a grant policy. |
| `--revoke-policy-id string` | Revoke policy ID to bind; must exist and be a revoke policy. |
| `--certify-policy-id string` | Certify policy ID to bind; must exist and be a certify policy. |
| `--emergency-grant-policy-id string` | Emergency grant policy ID to bind; must exist and be a grant policy. |
| `--resource-type string` | Kind of resource type to create, case-insensitive: `role`, `group`, `license`, `project`, `catalog`, `custom`, `vault`, `profile-type`, `session-policy`, or `claw-agent` (default `custom`). |
| `--resource-type-display-name string` | Display name for the resource type this command creates (default: `--display-name`). |
| `--resource-type-id string` | Existing app resource type to reuse instead of creating one. |
| `--resource-display-name string` | Display name for the resource this command creates (default: `--display-name`). |
| `--resource-id string` | Existing app resource to reuse instead of creating one; requires `--resource-type-id`. |

Passing `--resource-type` or `--resource-type-display-name` together with `--resource-type-id` is a usage error (exit 2), as is `--resource-id` without `--resource-type-id`. Only the `CUSTOM` resource type can repeat on one app, so reuse an existing type with `--resource-type-id` for any other kind.

Before the first request, c1i checks each policy ID and each `--owner-id`. An unknown policy or user exits with code 4 and creates nothing; a policy that is deleted or of the wrong type exits with code 2. The owner check reads the user, so a credential without user-read access exits with code 3. c1i sends a repeated `--owner-id` once. `--dry-run` runs the same checks.

There is no rollback. If a later request fails, the objects that earlier ones created still exist; the error names them and the flags that reuse them. `--dry-run` previews every request in the sequence, and where an ID exists only after an earlier request, the preview shows `NEW_APP_RESOURCE_TYPE_ID` or `NEW_APP_RESOURCE_ID` in its place. Owner changes are applied asynchronously.

**Example:**

```bash
c1i entitlements create --app-id "$APP_ID" \
  --display-name "Payroll admin" --resource-type-display-name "Payroll role" \
  --slug member --alias payroll_admin
```

### `entitlements update`

Update an entitlement's display name, description, policies, or maximum grant duration. c1i builds the update mask from the flags you pass, so an omitted flag is left alone.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements update <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--display-name string` | New display name. Can't be blank. |
| `--description string` | New description. An empty value is sent, and the server restores the description the entitlement was created with (empty when it had none) — not a general clear. |
| `--grant-policy-id string` | Grant policy ID to bind; must exist and be a grant policy. |
| `--revoke-policy-id string` | Revoke policy ID to bind; must exist and be a revoke policy. |
| `--certify-policy-id string` | Certify policy ID to bind; must exist and be a certify policy. |
| `--emergency-grant-policy-id string` | Emergency grant policy ID to bind; must exist and be a grant policy. |
| `--emergency-grant-enabled` | Enable or disable emergency grants. Enabling requires `--emergency-grant-policy-id` in the same call. |
| `--unbind-policy stringArray` | Unbind a policy: `grant`, `revoke`, `certify`, or `emergency-grant` (repeatable). |
| `--duration-grant string` | Maximum grant duration, as a whole-number Go duration such as `8h` or a protobuf duration such as `3600s`; c1i sends it in seconds, so `8h` reads back as `28800s`. |
| `--duration-unset` | Remove the maximum grant duration, which gives standing access. Can't be combined with `--duration-grant`. |

Each policy ID is checked before anything is sent. An ID that doesn't exist exits with code 4; one that is deleted or of the wrong type exits with code 2. That check runs on every bind, so an `--emergency-grant-policy-id` that the server would otherwise store unchecked is refused too. An empty policy ID is a usage error rather than an unbind, so pass `--unbind-policy` to remove a policy. `--dry-run` runs the same check.

`--emergency-grant-enabled` needs `--emergency-grant-policy-id` in the same call, even when the entitlement already has a policy stored, or c1i exits with code 2. The server doesn't refuse unbinding the emergency policy while emergency grants stay enabled, so pass `--emergency-grant-enabled=false` alongside `--unbind-policy emergency-grant`.

The server holds the duration as one choice, so `--duration-grant` and `--duration-unset` are mutually exclusive. A blank `--display-name` exits `2` before sending: the server would restore the entitlement's original name instead.

The updated entitlement is returned as formatted JSON, unwrapped like `entitlements get`.

**Example:**

```bash
c1i entitlements update <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  --grant-policy-id <policy-id> --duration-grant 8h
```

### `entitlements delete`

Delete an app entitlement. The delete is soft: `entitlements get` still returns the entitlement with `deletedAt` set. `apps delete` leaves an app's entitlements live, so delete them with this command first. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements delete <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |

The server also answers `200 {}` for an unknown entitlement ID or for a `--app-id` the entitlement does not belong to, so the exit code alone doesn't prove a delete. Confirm with `entitlements get`.

### `entitlements grants`
