List every grant of one app entitlement, one NDJSON row per grant, with the account, the C1 user it maps to, and two times `grants list` does not show: `discovered_at` and `expires_at` (the API's `discovered` and `expired`). `expires_at` held the future end time set by [`grants update-duration`](#grants), and was null for a standing grant and once that time had passed. `grant_source_count` is the number of groups or roles the grant is inherited through (0 = direct), and `grant_reason_count` counts the API's `grantReason`s.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements grants <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits); `--page-size` is clamped to the server's 100.

A well-formed entitlement or app ID that doesn't exist returns zero rows and exits `0`, not `4`, so an empty result means either no grants or a wrong ID; `entitlements get` on the same IDs exits `4` and can tell you which.

### `entitlements owners`

List an entitlement's owners as NDJSON, in the same row shape as `apps owners`. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements owners <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--entitlements` | List the entitlements that own it instead of its users, from the v2 owner route (role `primary`). Rows carry the owning entitlement's `id`, `app_id`, `display_name`, `slug`, `alias`, `role_slug`, `added_at`, and `deleted_at`. |

Use this to verify an owner change. Owner changes are applied asynchronously and can take several minutes to appear. A well-formed entitlement or app ID that doesn't exist returns zero rows and exits `0`, so an empty result means either no owners or a wrong ID; with `--entitlements` an unknown ID exits `4`.

### `entitlements add-owner`

Add one user as an entitlement owner without changing the rest of the owner list. Adding a user who is already an owner exits `0`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements add-owner <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  (--user-id <user-id> | --owner-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> [--owner-entitlement-app-id <app-id>]) [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--user-id string` | C1 user ID to add as owner. One per call, at most once; use `set-owners` for several. |
| `--owner-entitlement-id string` | Entitlement whose grantees own this entitlement. Exactly one of this and `--user-id` is required. |
| `--owner-entitlement-app-id string` | App of `--owner-entitlement-id` when it differs from the owned entitlement's app (default: the owned entitlement's app). |
| `--wait` | Block and poll until the owner appears, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Maximum time to wait with `--wait` (default `4m0s`). |

An unknown user, entitlement, or app exits with code 4; the `--user-id` user is looked up before sending, `--dry-run` included, and that lookup needs user-read access (exit `3` without it). A `--wait` that reaches `--wait-timeout` exits with code 1, though the write was accepted. `--wait` progress goes to stderr, and the command prints the API's JSON response on stdout. With `--dry-run`, `--wait` never polls.

With `--owner-entitlement-id` the write goes through the v2 owner route under the role `primary` (the only role the API supports). That form takes no `--wait` (exit `2`): the owner is in place when the command returns. Adding an entitlement that is already an owner exits `0`; an unknown entitlement exits `4`.

### `entitlements remove-owner`

Remove one user as an entitlement owner without changing the rest of the owner list. Unlike `set-owners`, it can remove the last owner.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements remove-owner <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  (--user-id <user-id> | --owner-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> [--owner-entitlement-app-id <app-id>])
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--user-id string` | C1 user ID to remove as owner. One per call, at most once; use `set-owners` for several. |
| `--owner-entitlement-id string` | Entitlement whose grantees own this entitlement. Exactly one of this and `--user-id` is required. |
| `--owner-entitlement-app-id string` | App of `--owner-entitlement-id` when it differs from the owned entitlement's app (default: the owned entitlement's app). |

A user who was never an owner exits with code 4, the same as an unknown user, entitlement, or app, so exit 4 can't tell those cases apart. A user who was an owner and is already removed exits `0`. Removing an entitlement owner a second time exits `0`; an unknown entitlement exits `4`. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

c1i doesn't look the `--user-id` user up first, so the server decides and a `--dry-run` preview can pass an ID the real run refuses.

### `entitlements set-owners`

Set the complete list of owners for an entitlement, replacing any existing owners. It refuses an empty list, because the API treats an empty list as "remove every owner."

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements set-owners <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  (--user-id <user-id> | --owner-entitlement-id <entitlement-id>) [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--user-id stringArray` | C1 user ID to set as owner (repeatable; replaces the user owners). |
| `--owner-entitlement-id stringArray` | Entitlement whose grantees own this entitlement (repeatable; replaces both the user and the entitlement owners). |
| `--owner-entitlement-app-id string` | App of the `--owner-entitlement-id` values when it differs from the owned entitlement's app (default: the owned entitlement's app). |
| `--no-user-owners` | With `--owner-entitlement-id` and no `--user-id`, remove every user owner. |
| `--wait` | Block and poll until the owner set equals the requested owners, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. A timeout exits with code 1, though the write was accepted. |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Maximum time to wait with `--wait` (default `4m0s`). |

Before it sends the request, c1i looks up each distinct `--user-id`. An unknown user exits with code 4, naming the ID, and nothing is sent, because the API would accept the unknown ID and silently drop it, possibly leaving no owners. The lookup needs user-read access. `--dry-run` runs the same lookups. c1i sends a repeated `--user-id` once, because the API answers a duplicate with a 500.

With any `--owner-entitlement-id`, the set goes through the v2 owner route, which replaces the entitlement owners and is meant to replace the user owners with the `--user-id` values. Repeat `--user-id` for each user owner to keep, or pass `--no-user-owners` to remove them all; with neither, c1i refuses (exit `2`) and sends nothing. `--no-user-owners` needs `--owner-entitlement-id` and excludes `--user-id`. Without `--owner-entitlement-id`, the command replaces user owners only and leaves entitlement owners in place.

Owner changes are applied asynchronously. `--wait` polls the v1 owner ID list — and, in the entitlement form, the v2 entitlement owner list too — until both equal the request, removals included. `--wait` progress goes to stderr, and the command prints the API's JSON response on stdout.

### `entitlements automation`

Manage the automation rule of an entitlement: per the API, it "automatically provision[s] or revoke[s] access based on defined conditions". An entitlement has at most one, and only an entitlement of the C1 app takes one (access profiles, groups, and similar) — any other app's entitlement is refused with a 400 (exit `2`, `only the C1 app can have entitlement automation`).

The condition is one of three forms: a CEL expression over the user (`--cel`), a JSON array of `{"appId","id"}` entitlement references whose holders match (`--refs-file`, 1 to 32 references), or none (`--none`). Each referenced entitlement is looked up first: the server stores an unknown one without complaint, so c1i exits `4` instead, `--dry-run` included. Test a CEL expression with [`policies validate-cel`](#policies-validate-cel) first; the server refuses a malformed one with a 400.

An automation acts on real users as soon as it exists; start with a condition that matches the users you mean.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i entitlements automation get <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Get the automation. The condition is under exactly one of `.cel`, `.entitlements`, `.none`, or `.basic`. |
| `c1i entitlements automation create <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> --display-name <name>` | Create the automation. |
| `c1i entitlements automation update <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Change its display name, description, or condition. |
| `c1i entitlements automation delete <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Soft-delete the automation. |
| `c1i entitlements automation exclusions list <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>` | List the C1 users the automation leaves alone. Rows: `user_id`, `display_name`, `email`, `status`. |
| `c1i entitlements automation exclusions add <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> --user-id <id>` | Add users to the exclusion list, one request for all IDs. |
| `c1i entitlements automation exclusions remove <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> --user-id <id>` | Remove users from the exclusion list, one request for all IDs. |

`create` takes `--display-name` (the server requires 3 to 512 bytes), an optional `--description`, and at most one condition flag; with none, the server stores an automation without a condition. `--body-file` sends an `AppEntitlementAutomation` object as `{"automation": <object>}` instead, and is the only way to set the `basic` condition. The server refuses a second automation on the same entitlement with a 409 (exit `2`), and in testing it refused the same way after the first was deleted, with retrying never helping.

`update` derives its mask from the flags you pass, and a condition flag replaces the whole condition; `--description ""` clears it. The server requires a display name on every update, even one that does not change it, so without `--display-name` c1i reads the automation and sends its current name. Setting the `basic` condition is not supported here; use `c1i api`.

`get` exits `4` for an entitlement with no automation (the server answers 404) and for an unknown entitlement. Every other automation command takes `--app-id` as the C1 app's ID.

The delete is soft: `get` still returns the automation with `deletedAt` set, a repeated delete exits `0`, and `update` also succeeds against a deleted one, printing it with `deletedAt` still set — so exit `0` does not mean the rule is live. The exclusion list can be read and changed whether or not the entitlement has a live automation; in testing `list`, `add`, and `remove` all exited `0` against a deleted one.

`exclusions add` and `exclusions remove` take repeatable `--user-id` and require at least one. The server answers an unknown ID with `200` and stores nothing, so c1i looks each one up first: an unknown ID exits `4` and nothing is sent. Adding a user already excluded, or removing one who is not, exits `0`. Both print `{}`. `exclusions list` exits `4` for an unknown entitlement and [paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

**Example:**

```bash
c1i entitlements automation create <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  --display-name "Engineering" --cel 'subject.department == "Engineering"'
```

### `entitlements account-provisioning`

Manage `provisionerPolicy` on an app entitlement: the account-provisioning configuration shown in the Console under App > Accounts management > Provisioning. This family covers exactly the `connector.account` arm — provisioning through the app's own connector, with CEL config fields. [`entitlements proxy-bindings`](#entitlements-proxy-bindings) plus the `delegate-entitlement-provisioning` guide in [`docs guide`](#docs-guide) cover the separate `delegated` arm, which has no first-class command.

The entitlement update endpoint takes the whole entitlement plus an `updateMask`; a request that sends `provisionerPolicy` with that mask but omits the account-provisioning object still answers `200` and **wipes** the stored policy. `set` always builds and sends the complete policy with `updateMask=provisionerPolicy`, so it cannot send that empty body. Run `show` first to see what is stored before changing it.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i entitlements account-provisioning show <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id>` | Show the stored `provisionerPolicy`. |
| `c1i entitlements account-provisioning set <entitlement-id> --app-id <app-id> --connector-id <id> --field <key=CEL> (--save-to-vault-id <id> \| --do-not-save)` | Set the connector account-provisioning policy. |

`show` prints `.provisionerPolicy` alone, not the whole entitlement (`entitlements get` prints that); it is named `show`, not `get`, because the policy has no ID of its own to guarantee at the top level. An entitlement with no provisioning configured prints `{}` (the field absent or null), or the explicit `{"unconfigured": {}}` the API stores once a policy is wiped; the policy is printed as stored, not normalized. A `200` whose response does not hold the entitlement exits `6`, so a broken response cannot pass for "nothing configured". `--fields` projects within this object, the same rule as every other read.

`set` flags:

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--connector-id string` | Connector that provisions the account; must be a live connector of `--app-id`. (Required.) |
| `--field stringArray` | Account-schema config field as `key=CEL` (repeatable; at least one). (Required.) |
| `--save-to-vault-id stringArray` | Vault (credential store) ID to save a new account's starter password to (repeatable). Required unless `--do-not-save`. |
| `--do-not-save` | Don't save a new account's starter password to a vault. Required unless `--save-to-vault-id`. |
| `--test` | Evaluate every `--field` expression with the account-provision policy tester before writing; abort on the first one the server rejects. |

Exactly one of `--save-to-vault-id` or `--do-not-save` chooses whether the starter password the connector returns for a new account is saved to a credential store. `--connector-id` is read first, `--dry-run` included: an unknown one exits `4`, a deleted one `2`.

`--test` evaluates each `--field` expression with the same route as `c1i policies test-provision`, in the order given, before anything is written — `--dry-run` included — and stops at the first one the server rejects, naming its key. The server answers a CEL expression that does not compile with a 500 (exit `6`), not a 4xx.

`set` prints the updated entitlement, unwrapped like `entitlements get`, so the ID is `.id`.

### `entitlements proxy-bindings`

A proxy binding is a visibility and tracking link from a source entitlement to a destination entitlement — it does not grant access or configure delegated provisioning. The public REST API has no list endpoint for proxy bindings; the C1 Console discovers them through an internal search service that isn't part of the public API.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `c1i entitlements proxy-bindings get` | Get an entitlement proxy binding. |
| `c1i entitlements proxy-bindings create` | Create an entitlement proxy binding. |
| `c1i entitlements proxy-bindings delete` | Delete an entitlement proxy binding. |

Each subcommand takes the same four flags to identify the binding:

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--source-app-id string` | Application ID of the source entitlement. (Required.) |
| `--source-entitlement-id string` | Source entitlement ID. (Required.) |
| `--destination-app-id string` | Application ID of the destination entitlement. (Required.) |
| `--destination-entitlement-id string` | Destination entitlement ID. (Required.) |

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i entitlements proxy-bindings get --source-app-id <app-id> \
  --source-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> --destination-app-id <app-id> \
  --destination-entitlement-id <entitlement-id>
c1i entitlements proxy-bindings create --source-app-id <app-id> \
  --source-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> --destination-app-id <app-id> \
  --destination-entitlement-id <entitlement-id>
c1i entitlements proxy-bindings delete --source-app-id <app-id> \
  --source-entitlement-id <entitlement-id> --destination-app-id <app-id> \
  --destination-entitlement-id <entitlement-id>
```

## Grants

Query access grants, the bindings between accounts or users and entitlements, to answer "who has access to what". You can also change them directly: add a user to a manually managed entitlement, remove one account's membership, and set or clear when a grant is revoked automatically.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i grants list`](#grants-list) | Search and list grants. |
| [`c1i grants accounts`](#grants-accounts) | List which of a C1 user's accounts in an app hold an entitlement. |
| [`c1i grants add`](#grants-add) | Grant users a manually managed entitlement directly, bypassing request policy. |
| [`c1i grants remove`](#grants-remove) | Remove one account's membership of an entitlement directly. |
| [`c1i grants update-duration`](#grants-update-duration-and-remove-duration) | Set when an existing grant is revoked automatically. |
| [`c1i grants remove-duration`](#grants-update-duration-and-remove-duration) | Make an existing grant standing, with no automatic revocation. |

A grant row is not durable: the search returned a grant with `entitlement_deleted_at: null` immediately after its entitlement's delete, then omitted it about 15 seconds later. Record `identity_user_id` before deleting an entitlement if you need the inventory.

### `grants list`

Search and list grants. At least one filter is required. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Filter to grants in this application. |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Filter to grants of this entitlement (requires `--app-id`). |
| `--user-id string` | Filter to grants held by this C1 identity user. |
| `--app-user-id string` | Filter to grants held by this app account (app user). |
| `--wait` | Block and poll every page until the same grants come back `--wait-stable` times in a row, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. |
| `--wait-stable int` | Consecutive identical reads that `--wait` requires before printing (minimum 2; default 3). |
| `--wait-min int` | Minimum matching grants that `--wait` requires before it can settle (0 = an empty result can settle). |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Maximum time to wait with `--wait` (default `4m0s`). |

Each row includes the entitlement, the account (`app_user_*`) and its `identity_user_id`, timestamps (`created_at`, `deprovision_at`), and `grant_source_count` — `0` for a direct grant, or the number of groups or roles the access is inherited through. Rows also carry `entitlement_deleted_at` and `app_user_deleted_at`; an absent timestamp is `null`, never `""`.

Grant provisioning is asynchronous, so a read taken right after a grant or revoke can catch the set mid-change. With `--wait`, c1i re-reads every page of the match every 5 seconds, regardless of `--limit`, and prints the settled set once the same grants come back `--wait-stable` times in a row; progress goes to stderr, so stdout stays pure NDJSON. The 5-second interval is fixed and is not a flag.

An empty result is stable: a filter matching nothing settles at the first opportunity, roughly 10 seconds at the defaults, and exits `0` with zero rows. If you are waiting for a grant you just made, pass `--wait-min 1` (or the count you expect) so the wait holds out for it instead of settling on zero rows. The default of 0 is deliberate: empty-and-stable is the correct answer when you are waiting for a revoke.

`--wait` settles on the whole matching set, so `--limit` only truncates what is printed. It cannot be combined with `--page-token`, and a `--wait-stable` that cannot fit inside `--wait-timeout` is a usage error rather than a guaranteed timeout.

**Example:**

```bash
# Who holds an entitlement?
c1i grants list --app-id <app-id> --entitlement-id <entitlement-id>

# What does a C1 identity have, across apps?
c1i grants list --user-id <user-id>
```

### `grants accounts`

List a C1 user's grants of one entitlement, one row per app account, which answers "which of this person's accounts in the app hold this entitlement" when a user has more than one account there.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i grants accounts --app-id <app-id> --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> --user-id <user-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Entitlement ID. (Required.) |
| `--user-id string` | C1 user ID whose accounts to list. (Required.) |
| `--limit int` | Cap the rows printed. The endpoint does not paginate. |

Rows carry `app_id`, `entitlement_id`, `app_user_id`, `created_at`, `deprovision_at` (null for a standing grant), `deleted_at`, and `grant_source_count` (0 = direct grant, as in `grants list`).

A well-formed user, entitlement, or app ID that doesn't exist returns zero rows and exits `0`, not `4`; [`users get`](#users) and [`entitlements get`](#entitlements-get) exit `4` for an ID that doesn't exist.

### `grants add`

Grant users a manually managed entitlement directly. This bypasses the entitlement's grant policy and approval, so no access-request task is created. For a policy-respecting request, use [`requests create grant`](#requests-create-grant) instead.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i grants add --app-id <app-id> \
  --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> --user-id <user-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Entitlement ID to grant. (Required.) |
| `--user-id stringArray` | C1 user ID to grant (repeatable; one request carries every ID). (Required.) |
| `--wait` | Block and poll until every accepted user holds the entitlement, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. A timeout exits `1`, though the write was accepted. |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Maximum time to wait with `--wait` (default `4m0s`). |

c1i looks up each `--user-id` before sending, including under `--dry-run`; an unknown user exits `4` and nothing is sent, and the lookup needs user-read access (exit `3` without it). A repeated `--user-id` is sent once. Adding a user who already holds the entitlement exits `0`. The server's `failedUsersErrorMap` is printed when present, each rejected user is named, and the command exits `1`; `--wait` then waits only for the users that were accepted.

The grant lands asynchronously — seconds in testing, though reads can lag. `--wait` polls the entitlement's grants until every accepted user appears, and `--dry-run` with `--wait` still only previews the write.

`grants remove` addresses a grant by its account, not by user, and refused manually managed entitlements in testing; see [`grants remove`](#grants-remove).

### `grants remove`

Remove one app account (app user) from an entitlement directly, the counterpart of `grants add` at the account level. `--app-user-id` is the `app_user_id` of [`grants list`](#grants-list).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i grants remove --app-id <app-id> --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> --app-user-id <app-user-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Entitlement ID to remove the account from. (Required.) |
| `--app-user-id string` | App account (app user) ID to remove. (Required.) |

Per the API, the command covers a manually managed entitlement, C1 catalogs, groups and profile types, an SSO application's sign-in entitlement (removing only direct manual access), and an access profile on a tenant with the JML feature. In testing, the server refused a manually managed entitlement of the custom and group resource types — an account granted with `grants add` — with a 400 (exit `2`, `entitlement is not an SSO application sign-in entitlement`). Only those refusals were observed; a successful removal has not been exercised, so treat exit `0` as the server's answer and confirm with `grants list`.

An unknown entitlement or app exits `4`. The server checks the entitlement before the account, so on a refused entitlement an unknown `--app-user-id` earns the same 400. The response's `revokeTaskId` is set only when the entitlement is an access profile, per the API.

The policy-respecting removal is [`requests create revoke`](#requests-create-revoke); on a manual entitlement its task waits on a provisioning step, and the grant stays until that step is completed.

### `grants update-duration` and `remove-duration`

Set or clear when C1 revokes an existing grant automatically. Both address one grant by its account — the `app_user_id` of [`grants list`](#grants-list) — and both print the grant as formatted JSON, so the end time is `.deprovisionAt`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i grants update-duration --app-id <app-id> --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> \
  --app-user-id <app-user-id> --deprovision-at 2026-12-01T00:00:00Z
c1i grants remove-duration --app-id <app-id> --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> \
  --app-user-id <app-user-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID the entitlement belongs to. (Required.) |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Entitlement ID of the grant. (Required.) |
| `--app-user-id string` | App account (app user) ID holding the grant. (Required.) |
| `--deprovision-at string` | (`update-duration` only) When to revoke the grant, as an RFC3339 timestamp such as `2026-12-01T00:00:00Z`. Must be in the future. (Required.) |

`--deprovision-at` must parse as RFC3339; anything else exits `2` before sending. The server refuses a time in the past with a 400 (exit `2`, `new_deprovision_at must be in the future`).

At the end time C1 starts the revoke. In testing on a manually managed entitlement, `update-duration` opened a revoke task (`auto created for deprovision`) and the grant stayed listed with `deprovision_at` null while that task was open.

`remove-duration` clears the end time, so the grant is no longer revoked automatically and the printed `.deprovisionAt` is null. The change shows as `deprovision_at` in `grants list` and as `expires_at` in [`entitlements grants`](#entitlements-grants).

An unknown app, entitlement, or account, or an account without this grant, exits `4` from the server. For a grant still being requested, use [`tasks update-grant-duration`](#tasks-update-grant-duration) instead.

## Tasks

List access request tasks and act on them: approve, deny, comment, reassign, escalate, or move them through their policy, and read a task's audit trail.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i tasks list`](#tasks-list) | Search and list tasks. |
| [`c1i tasks get`](#tasks-get) | Get a single task by ID. |
| [`c1i tasks audits`](#tasks-audits) | List a task's audit trail events. |
| [`c1i tasks approve`](#tasks-approve) | Approve a task. |
| [`c1i tasks deny`](#tasks-deny) | Deny a task. |
| [`c1i tasks comment`](#tasks-comment) | Add a comment to a task. |
| [`c1i tasks close`](#tasks-close) | Close a task without approving or denying it. |
| [`c1i tasks escalate`](#tasks-escalate) | Escalate a grant task to its entitlement's emergency access policy. |
| [`c1i tasks process`](#tasks-process) | Process a task now rather than waiting for the next cycle. |
| [`c1i tasks reassign`](#tasks-reassign) | Reassign a task's approval step to one or more other users. |
| [`c1i tasks reset`](#tasks-reset) | Hard-reset a task to the start of its policy. |
| [`c1i tasks restart`](#tasks-restart) | Restart a task's approval step, sending it back for a fresh decision. |
| [`c1i tasks retry-provisioning`](#tasks-retry-provisioning) | Retry a task's failed connector provisioning. |
| [`c1i tasks skip-step`](#tasks-skip-step) | Skip the task's current policy step, advancing the policy without a decision. A current provision step is refused. |
| [`c1i tasks update-grant-duration`](#tasks-update-grant-duration) | Change the grant duration a task will provision. |
| [`c1i tasks update-request-data`](#tasks-update-request-data) | Submit form data for a task at a form step. |

### `tasks list`

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--state string` | Filter by state: `open`, `closed`, or the full `TASK_STATE_*` name of one. |
| `--query string` | Search task display name or description. |
| `--assigned-to-me` | Only show tasks assigned to the current user. |

### `tasks get`

Get a single task by ID: the full task view, including its current policy step and outcome, as formatted JSON. It reads the same endpoint as `c1i requests get`, because access requests are tasks. Use it to re-read a task after an action, since action output reports the task's state from before the action.

A `200` that doesn't carry the task exits with code 6 and prints nothing, rather than printing an empty object at exit `0`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks get <task-id>
```

### `tasks audits`

List the audit trail of one task — creation, approvals, comments, state changes, and provisioning events — oldest first, as NDJSON.

Each row is one event as the API returns it. Its kind is the one event key it carries besides the common ones (for example `.taskCreated`, `.comment`, `.stateChange`, `.grantOutcome`); `.eventType`, `.currentState`, `.created`, and `.userId` are on every row.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks audits <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--newest-first` | Newest event first (default: oldest first). |
| `--comments-only` | Only comment events. |
| `--exclude-comments` | Drop comment events. Combining it with `--comments-only` exits with code 2. |

c1i reads the task first, because the audit endpoint answers an unknown task ID with an empty list; an unknown task therefore exits with code 4 instead of printing nothing. That read accepts a numeric task ID, and the 27-character ID it returns is the one sent to the audit route.

The audit search is a POST that c1i treats as a read: a `5xx` is retried like other list reads.

### `tasks approve`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks approve <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the approval. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Policy step to approve (defaults to the task's current step). |

If you omit `--policy-step-id`, c1i fetches and uses the task's currently executing step. If the task has no current step, the command exits with code 2 and tells you to re-read the task with `c1i tasks get`; `--policy-step-id` can't supply a step the task doesn't have.

### `tasks deny`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks deny <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the denial. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Policy step to deny (defaults to the task's current step). |

`deny` resolves the current policy step the same way `approve` does. A step it looked up is reported on stderr (`Resolved policy step: <id>`), not on stdout.

A task with no current step — for a moment right after creation, before its policy materializes — is refused by the server (exit `2`; the message varies, for example a missing-step validation error or `already approved`), so retry once the task shows a step. A task that can't be fetched is an error (exit `4` for an unknown ID), never a silent denial without a step.

### `tasks comment`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks comment <task-id> --comment <comment>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment text. (Required.) |

### `tasks close`

Closing cancels the task and records no approval decision; use `approve` or `deny` to record an outcome.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks close <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the close. |

### `tasks escalate`

Move a grant task onto the emergency-grant policy of the entitlement it requests, in place of the normal approval flow. Afterwards the task's `.emergencyAccess` is true and its current step is that policy's first step.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks escalate <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the escalation. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Policy step to escalate from (defaults to the task's current step). |

The server allows it only while the task's `.actions` lists `TASK_ACTION_TYPE_ESCALATE_TO_EMERGENCY_ACCESS`; otherwise, for example when the entitlement has no emergency-grant policy or the task was already escalated, it refuses with `action not permitted` (exit `2`).

The step is required by the server and defaults to the task's currently executing step. Because that step is fetched, `--dry-run` authenticates and issues a read against the tenant before printing its preview.

### `tasks process`

Use it on a task that looks stuck: it asks C1 to re-run policy evaluation without changing the task's approval state. The request body is empty, so it takes neither a comment nor a policy step.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks process <task-id>
```

On a healthy task nothing observable changes: state, current policy step, and history are all identical afterwards. The stalled case was not reproduced, so treat any effect there as unverified.

### `tasks reassign`

`--to-user-id` sets the step's approvers to the users you name; repeat it to assign several. The API would store an unknown ID as the step's approver, so c1i looks up each user first: an unknown ID exits with code 4 before anything is sent, and the credential needs user read (else exit `3`), `--dry-run` included. A repeated ID is sent once.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks reassign <task-id> --to-user-id <user-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--to-user-id stringArray` | User ID to reassign the step to (repeatable). (Required.) |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the reassignment. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Policy step to reassign (defaults to the task's current step). |

If a step already names a bad approver, re-run `tasks reassign` with valid IDs; a system owner can also use `tasks reset` or `tasks restart` where the task offers them.

### `tasks reset`

Unlike `restart`, which re-runs the current step, `reset` discards the task's approval progress and starts the policy over. It takes no policy step.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks reset <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the reset. |

On a closed task the result depends on how it closed: a `CANCELLED`, `GRANTED`, or `REVOKED` task reopens (exit `0`, state `TASK_STATE_OPEN`, outcome cleared), while a `DENIED` one exits `0` and stays closed with outcome `DENIED` — no visible change. Neither is a general undo; re-read with `c1i tasks get`.

### `tasks restart`

On an open task, this rotates the current policy step and records the restart in the task's policy history. Whether `restart` is available depends on the task; the server otherwise refuses with `action not permitted`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks restart <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the restart. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Policy step to restart (defaults to the task's current step). |

On a closed task the result depends on how it closed: a `CANCELLED`- or `DENIED`-closed task is refused with `task is closed` (exit `2`), a `GRANTED`-closed one reopens (exit `0`, state `TASK_STATE_OPEN`, outcome cleared), and a `REVOKED` close was untested. Neither is a general undo; re-read with `c1i tasks get` rather than treating the exit as one.

`restart`, `reset`, and `skip-step` each rotate the current policy step, so a `--policy-step-id` captured before one of them goes stale and the server answers `this action is no longer available: the request has advanced to a new approval step`. Omit the flag to act on whatever step is current.

Because the step is fetched, `--dry-run` authenticates and issues a read against the tenant before printing its preview.

### `tasks retry-provisioning`

Retry the connector provisioning of a task whose provision step failed. The API describes it as resetting the failed connector actions and re-driving the connector while keeping the approvals already collected.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks retry-provisioning <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the retry. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Provision step to retry (defaults to the task's current step). |

The server allows it only while the task's `.actions` lists `TASK_ACTION_TYPE_RETRY_PROVISIONING`; on a task at an approval step it refuses with `action not permitted` (exit `2`). Because the current step is fetched, `--dry-run` authenticates and issues a read against the tenant before previewing.

### `tasks skip-step`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks skip-step <task-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--comment string` | Comment to include with the skip. |
| `--policy-step-id string` | Policy step to skip (defaults to the task's current step). |

`skip-step` refuses a current provision step (exit `2`, nothing sent): skipping it abandons provisioning and can leave the task stranded or close it. To skip one on purpose, name it with `--policy-step-id`; that explicit form is sent as given.

### `tasks update-grant-duration`

The task must still be at an approval step; once it reaches provisioning, the server refuses with `cannot update grant duration for a ticket in a provision step`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks update-grant-duration <task-id> --duration <duration>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--duration string` | Grant duration, as a whole-number Go duration such as `8h` or a protobuf duration such as `3600s`; c1i sends it in seconds. A sign, decimal, or leading zero (`-1h`, `1.5h`, `01h`) exits with code 2. (Required.) |

`0s` is sent, and the server has been seen storing it without complaint.

The new value lands at `.type.grant.grantDuration` on the task — there is no top-level `grantDuration` — so read it back with `c1i requests get <task-id>`.

### `tasks update-request-data`

Submit the request form of a task at a form step. The command sends form data; a successful submission has not been observed through c1i.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i tasks update-request-data <task-id> --data-file <file>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--data-file string` | Form data as a JSON object (file, or `-` for stdin). A missing, malformed, `null`, or non-object file exits with code 2 before sending. (Required.) |

The form's field names are at `.policy.current.form.form.fields[].name` in `c1i tasks get`. What was observed: an object that doesn't fit the form gets a schema error naming fields by display name (`request data does not match the required schema (field 'Reason' is required)`); a valid object on a self-requested task at a form step, whose `.actions` didn't list `TASK_ACTION_TYPE_UPDATE_REQUEST_DATA`, was refused with `action not permitted`; and a task at another step is refused with `current policy step is not a form instance`. All three exit `2`.

The task is not read first, so `<task-id>` must be the 27-character ID; the numeric ID that `tasks get` accepts is rejected by this route.

<Note>
Every task action prints the task as formatted JSON, unwrapped like `tasks get`, so the task's ID is `.id`. The action endpoints return the task as it was before the action, so `.state` is a snapshot, not the outcome: closing an open task prints an open state. Re-read the task with `c1i tasks get` before you act on it. When c1i looks up a policy step for you, it reports the step on stderr (`Resolved policy step: <id>`) rather than in the JSON. Actions that look up the current policy step — `approve`, `deny`, `reassign`, `restart`, `skip-step`, `escalate`, and `retry-provisioning` — send the task ID that lookup returns, so a numeric task ID that `tasks get` accepts also works for them.
</Note>

## Requests

File grant, revoke, action, and resource-action requests, track their status, and optionally wait for the created grant.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i requests list`](#requests-list) | List access requests and their status. |
| `c1i requests get <request-id>` | Get a single access request by ID. Access requests are backed by tasks, so the ID is the task ID that `c1i requests create` returns or `c1i requests list` shows. Outputs the full task view, including its current policy step and outcome, as formatted JSON. |
| [`c1i requests create grant`](#requests-create-grant) | Create a grant access request, optionally waiting for a new grant and targeting one app account. |
| [`c1i requests create revoke`](#requests-create-revoke) | Create a revoke access request, optionally targeting one app account. |
| [`c1i requests create action`](#requests-create-action) | Request one of the tenant's request actions (requestable automations). |
| [`c1i requests create resource-action`](#requests-create-resource-action) | Request a connector resource-create action, such as creating a group from a template. |

### `requests list`

List access requests: the grant and revoke tasks a requester files. By default it shows requests you opened or are the subject of, which complements `c1i tasks list` (the approver's view). [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--all` | List every access request in the tenant, not just yours. |
| `--user-id string` | Scope to requests opened by or about this user (default: you). |
| `--app-id string` | Filter to requests targeting this application. |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Filter to requests for this entitlement. Requires `--app-id`, because an entitlement ID repeats across apps. |
| `--type string` | Filter by request type: `grant` or `revoke` (default: both). |
| `--state string` | Filter by state: `open`, `closed`, or the full `TASK_STATE_*` name of one. |

### `requests create grant`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i requests create grant --app-id <app-id> \
  --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Entitlement ID. (Required.) |
| `--user-id string` | User ID (defaults to current user). |
| `--app-user-id string` | Application account (app user) ID to grant; use it instead of `--user-id` when the user has several accounts in the app. With both, the server requires the account to belong to the user. |
| `--duration string` | Requested access duration, as a whole-number Go duration such as `24h` or a protobuf duration such as `86400s`; c1i sends it in seconds. A sign, decimal, or leading zero exits with code 2, and so does a day unit such as `7d`. Zero is sent, and the server refuses it with `value must be greater than 0s`. |
| `--description string` | Justification for the request. |
| `--emergency` | Request emergency access. |
| `--wait` | Block until a new grant for this request appears in `grants list` and the set settles, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. A timeout exits `1`, though the task was created. |
| `--wait-min int` | Minimum matching grants before the wait can settle (default `1`; a new grant is always required, so `0` cannot settle on an empty set). |
| `--wait-stable int` | Consecutive identical reads before the wait settles (minimum 2; default `3`; reads are 5 seconds apart). |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Maximum time to wait with `--wait` (default `4m0s`). |

With `--wait`, the task is printed immediately, progress goes to stderr, and c1i reads the matching grant set before sending. A grant the recipient already held does not satisfy the wait; the command waits for a grant that was not there before, so a recipient who already holds the entitlement waits to the timeout. A request that needs approval therefore waits to the timeout too. A timeout exits `1`, though the request was accepted; re-read the task with `requests get`.

`--dry-run` never polls.

### `requests create revoke`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i requests create revoke --app-id <app-id> \
  --entitlement-id <entitlement-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--entitlement-id string` | Entitlement ID. (Required.) |
| `--user-id string` | User ID (defaults to current user). |
| `--app-user-id string` | Application account (app user) ID to revoke; use it instead of `--user-id` when the user has several accounts in the app. With both, the server requires the account to belong to the user. |
| `--description string` | Justification for the request. |

### `requests create action`

Create an action task: request one of the tenant's request actions, which are requestable automations.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i requests create action --action-id <action-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--action-id string` | ID of the request action to run. (Required.) |
| `--description string` | Description of the request. |
| `--form-values-file string` | Form values as a JSON object (file, or `-` for stdin). |
| `--group-only` | Refuse unless the action is a current group-creation template. |

The API has no route that lists or creates these actions, so neither c1i nor `c1i api` can look an ID up; an unknown ID exits `4` (the server answers `404 action not found`). `--group-only` sends the API's `groupOnly`, which the API documents as rejecting the request with `FAILED_PRECONDITION` unless the action is a current group-creation template.

### `requests create resource-action`

Create a task that runs a connector resource-create action, for example creating a group in a connected app from a group template.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i requests create resource-action --action-id <action-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--action-id string` | ID of the resource-create action to run. (Required.) |
| `--form-values-file string` | Form values as a JSON object (file, or `-` for stdin). The API validates them against the connector's schema at request time. |

As with `requests create action`, no API route lists these actions, so an unknown ID exits `4` (`action not found`).

All four `create` commands print the created task unwrapped like `requests get`, so the task's ID is `.id`, and its `.state` is a snapshot from creation rather than the outcome. A `--dry-run` preview carries no ID, so extract it with `jq -er`, which fails there, rather than `jq -r`, which prints `null` and exits `0`.

`create grant` and `create revoke` resolve the caller when you omit `--user-id`, so a `--dry-run` preview then needs credentials. An explicit `--user-id` is looked up before sending; an unknown user exits with code `4`, including under `--dry-run`.

## Connectors

Manage connectors: the per-app objects that sync a system's accounts, entitlements, and grants into C1, run provisioning, and back an MCP server. A connector belongs to exactly one app, so almost every command takes `--app-id` plus the connector ID positionally. `connectors debuggable` is the exception — it lists connectors across all apps and takes no scope flag.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i connectors list`](#connectors-list) | List an app's connectors. |
| [`c1i connectors get`](#connectors-get) | Get a single connector, including its sync status and config. |
| [`c1i connectors create`](#connectors-create) | Create a connector from a catalog entry, with an empty config. |
| [`c1i connectors update`](#connectors-update) | Update a connector from a JSON object plus an explicit update mask. |
| [`c1i connectors delete`](#connectors-delete) | Soft-delete a connector. |
| [`c1i connectors pause`](#connectors-pause) | Pause syncing and provisioning. |
| [`c1i connectors resume`](#connectors-resume) | Resume syncing and provisioning. |
| [`c1i connectors schedule`](#connectors-schedule) | Set or clear the connector's sync schedule. |
| [`c1i connectors sync`](#connectors-sync) | Start a sync now, optionally waiting for it. |
| [`c1i connectors sync-status`](#connectors-sync-status) | Show current sync progress. |
| [`c1i connectors debuggable`](#connectors-debuggable) | List the connectors you can run sync diagnostics on. |
| [`c1i connectors sync-history`](#connectors-sync-history) | Show recent sync runs and the failure trend. |
| [`c1i connectors sync-errors`](#connectors-sync-errors) | Show a digest of recent sync errors. |
| [`c1i connectors sync-changes`](#connectors-sync-changes) | Show a metadata-only timeline of changes around the connector. |
| [`c1i connectors sync-compare`](#connectors-sync-compare) | Compare the counts of two sync runs. |
| [`c1i connectors credentials get`](#connectors-credentials-get) | Get a connector credential. |
| [`c1i connectors credentials rotate`](#connectors-credentials-rotate) | Issue a new connector credential. |
| [`c1i connectors credentials revoke`](#connectors-credentials-revoke) | Revoke a connector credential. |

`connectors list` and `connectors debuggable` output NDJSON. `get`, `create`, `update`, and the `credentials` reads output the object unwrapped, so the ID is `.id`. `delete`, `pause`, `resume`, `schedule`, `sync`, and `credentials revoke` print the API's empty `{}`, where exit `0` is the success signal.

A connector's sync state lives in its `.status` object. `connectors get` shows it as `.status`, and `.status.status` is the sync status: `SYNC_STATUS_UNSPECIFIED` before the first sync, `SYNC_STATUS_RUNNING` mid-sync, `SYNC_STATUS_DONE` or `SYNC_STATUS_ERROR` at the end. `connectors sync-status` and `connectors sync --wait` print that object on its own, with the current step, live object counts, and `.lastError`.

Sync diagnostics are read-only except for `sync`. To work through a failing sync in order — find the connector, read its state, read the runs, then the error digest, the change timeline, and a run-to-run comparison — run `c1i docs guide diagnose-connector-sync`. Use [`c1i docs guide`](#docs-guide) for the full list of guides.

### `connectors list`

List connectors for an application. [Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

Rows are `id`, `app_id`, `display_name`, `status` (the sync status, such as `SYNC_STATUS_DONE`), and `deleted_at`. `deleted_at` is `null` for a live connector.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors list --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

### `connectors get`

Get a single connector by its ID. Output is the connector itself, not the API's `connectorView` wrapper: the ID is `.id`, `.status.status` is the sync status, and `config` is printed as the API returns it.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors get <connector-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

A `200` whose body holds no connector exits with code `6` and prints nothing, rather than an empty object.

### `connectors create`

Create a connector on an app from a catalog entry. The connector is created with an empty config — the API's delegated create — so set the config afterwards with [`connectors update`](#connectors-update) and `--update-mask config`.

c1i looks the app up first: an unknown `--app-id` exits `4`, and a deleted one exits `2`, because the server would otherwise store the connector under either. `--catalog-id` is required unless you use `--body-file`. A malformed catalog ID is a `400` (exit `2`); a well-formed ID the catalog lacks can answer `500 invalid connector catalog id` (exit `6`). An MCP catalog ID is refused with a `400` — `the MCP catalog is not installable; register an MCP server through the MCP server API` (exit `2`); use [`c1i mcp servers register`](#mcp-servers-register) instead.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors create --app-id <app-id> --catalog-id <catalog-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID to create the connector on. (Required.) |
| `--catalog-id string` | Catalog entry ID the connector is an instance of. (Required unless `--body-file`.) |
| `--display-name string` | Display name. Can't be blank. |
| `--description string` | Description. |
| `--user-id stringArray` | C1 user ID to set on the connector (repeatable). In the run checked, omitting it left the caller as the connector's only user ID. |
| `--body-file string` | Full `CreateDelegated` request JSON, sent verbatim (file, or `-` for stdin), for fields with no flag. Can't be combined with the other flags except `--app-id`, and must not set `appId` or `app_id` (exit `2`). |

Each `--user-id` is looked up before sending: an unknown ID exits `4` with nothing sent, and the write needs user read access (else exit `3`), including under `--dry-run`.

The created connector prints as pretty JSON, unwrapped like `connectors get`: the ID is `.id`.

### `connectors update`

Update a connector by sending a connector object with `--body-file` plus an explicit `--update-mask`; both are required. The object is sent verbatim with `id` set to `<connector-id>`.

Start from `connectors get` output with the keys it adds beside the connector removed — `expanded`, `appPath`, `capabilitiesPath`, and `usersPath` — because the server rejects those as unknown fields with a `400`. Editing `.config` this way is how a connector made by `connectors create` gets configured:

```bash
c1i connectors get <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  | jq 'del(.expanded, .appPath, .capabilitiesPath, .usersPath)' > conn.json
# edit .config in conn.json
c1i connectors update <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  --body-file conn.json --update-mask config
```

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors update <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  --body-file <file> --update-mask <fields>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--body-file string` | Connector JSON object, sent verbatim (file, or `-` for stdin). Requires `--update-mask`; must not contain `id`, or must equal `<connector-id>` (exit `2`). |
| `--update-mask string` | Comma-separated camelCase field paths to apply. (Required with `--body-file`.) |
| `--justification string` | Reason recorded for the change, sent as `justification.reason`. |

Mask paths are camelCase; a snake_case path is a `400` (exit `2`).

<Warning>
In the runs checked, the server required the object to carry `config` (else `400 connector: config is required`) and `catalogId` (else `500 cannot upgrade app connector version, catalog id is required`) whatever the mask named, and it validated `config` against the catalog entry's schema. A mask of `displayName` or `description` alone returned `200` without changing either field. Read the connector back to confirm a change.

A connector that backs an MCP server is refused by the server: its config type isn't one this endpoint accepts. Use [`c1i mcp servers update`](#mcp-servers-update) and [`c1i mcp servers update-credentials`](#mcp-servers-update-credentials) for those.
</Warning>

The updated connector prints unwrapped like `connectors get` (ID: `.id`).

### `connectors delete`

Delete a connector. c1i reads the connector first, under `--dry-run` too: an unknown ID exits `4` with nothing sent.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors delete <connector-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

A connector that backs an MCP server exits `2` with nothing sent. Deleting it here removed the server but left its toolsets and their entitlements live, so use [`c1i mcp servers delete`](#mcp-servers-delete), which removes those too.

The delete is soft: in the run checked, a deleted connector stayed readable with `connectors get` (`.deletedAt` set), and deleting it again exited `0`. Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

### `connectors pause`

Pause syncing and provisioning for a connector. The API documents that no new syncs or grant and revoke operations run until [`c1i connectors resume`](#connectors-resume). While paused, `connectors get` shows `.syncDisabledAt` set and `.canResumeSync` true.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors pause <connector-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

Pausing a connector that is already paused exits `0`. Prints `{}`.

### `connectors resume`

Resume syncing and provisioning for a paused connector. The API documents that resuming also starts a new sync, so read [`c1i connectors sync-status`](#connectors-sync-status) afterwards to follow it.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors resume <connector-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

Resuming a connector that isn't paused exits `0`. Prints `{}`.

### `connectors schedule`

Set a connector's sync schedule to a cron expression, or clear it.

```bash
c1i connectors schedule <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  --cron "0 */6 * * *" --timezone America/Chicago
c1i connectors schedule <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> --clear
```

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors schedule <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  (--cron <expression> [--timezone <iana-name>] | --clear)
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--cron string` | Cron expression for the sync schedule. (Required unless `--clear`.) |
| `--timezone string` | IANA timezone for `--cron`, such as `America/Chicago`. Omitted, none is sent and `connectors get` shows it as `""`. |
| `--clear` | Remove the sync schedule. Can't be combined with `--cron` or `--timezone` (exit `2`). |
| `--justification string` | Reason recorded for the change, sent as `justification.reason`. |

c1i doesn't validate `--cron` or `--timezone`, and in the run checked the server accepted values that are neither a cron expression nor an IANA name. `--clear` sends no schedule, and `connectors get` then shows `.connectorSyncCronSchedule` as `null`. Prints `{}`.

### `connectors sync`

Start a sync of a connector now. The API queues the sync, so exit `0` means the request was accepted, not that the sync finished. Follow progress with [`c1i connectors sync-status`](#connectors-sync-status). On a paused connector the API can accept the call but queue nothing; resume the connector first with [`c1i connectors resume`](#connectors-resume).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors sync <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--wait` | Block and poll `GET /api/v1/apps/{app-id}/connectors/{id}` until a new sync's `status.completedAt` appears, or `--wait-timeout` elapses. |
| `--wait-timeout duration` | Maximum time to wait with `--wait` (for example `30s` or `5m`); reaching it exits `1` (default `15m0s`). |

Without `--wait`, the command prints the API's `{}`.

`--wait` reads the connector's current `.status.completedAt` just before sending the request, then polls the same read as `connectors get` until a terminal status carries a later `completedAt`. A sync that was already done when the command ran and hasn't advanced yet therefore can't look like this sync finished. On success it prints the connector's whole `.status` object as this command's document, in place of the API's `{}`.

| Outcome | Exit code |
| :--- | :--- |
| A new sync reached a terminal state | `0`, with the `.status` object on stdout. |
| The sync ended in `SYNC_STATUS_ERROR` or with `.status.lastError` set | `1`, naming the status and the error; `applied: true`, because the sync was still accepted. |
| `--wait-timeout` elapsed | `1` with `applied: true`; the sync may still be running. |
| The read answered `200` without `connectorView.connector` | `6`, rather than waiting. |

A status that never advances `.status.completedAt` is reported at the timeout, not as a sync error: an unconfigured connector stays `SYNC_STATUS_DONE` with null timestamps, and a sync the server refuses before it starts stays `RUNNING`, so the wait can't tell either from a sync that hasn't started. `--dry-run` never polls and never reads the baseline.

### `connectors sync-status`

Show a connector's current sync progress: status, step, live object counts, the last error, and whether syncing is paused (`.syncDisabledReason`).

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors sync-status <connector-id> --app-id <app-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |

Output is the API's `status` object itself, not the connector — the same object `connectors sync --wait` prints. It prints what the server sent, so a connector that has never synced shows `SYNC_STATUS_UNSPECIFIED` with null timestamps rather than an error.

### `connectors debuggable`

List the connectors you may run sync diagnostics on, across all apps: one row per connector with `appId`, `connectorId`, `displayName`, `catalogId`, `authored`, `syncStatus`, and `lastCompletedAt`, as the API returns them. Take the `appId` and `connectorId` from a row and pass them to [`sync-history`](#connectors-sync-history) and the other `sync-*` commands.

Rows keep the API's camelCase field names, unlike most NDJSON list rows. An empty list means you may diagnose none of the tenant's connectors.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors debuggable [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--limit int` | Cap the total number of results; `0` is unlimited. |
| `--page-size int` | Rows to request per API page (max `50` here, unlike the usual `100`; the server answers `400` above it). |
| `--page-token string` | Pagination cursor. A non-empty value fetches exactly one page. |

Per the API description a page can be empty while more rows remain, and c1i keeps following the cursor.

### `connectors sync-history`

Show a connector's recent sync runs, newest first, and the failure trend within that window: `.streak` carries `failedRuns`, `finishedRuns`, `failureRatio`, and the first failure after the last success. Each run has its status, timing, `errorClass`, a redacted `errorSummary`, and, when recorded, `syncRunStats` object counts. The counts are `int64` values printed as JSON strings.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors sync-history <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--runs int` | Maximum runs to return. Max `50` (the server's documented bound); a larger value is clamped to `50`, a negative one exits `2`. `0` or unset leaves the server's default, which returned `20` in the runs checked. |

There is no next page: this is one bounded window, not a paginated listing. Output is the API's response object as returned. A run's `.lifecycleId` is what [`sync-compare`](#connectors-sync-compare) takes.

### `connectors sync-errors`

Show a bounded digest of a connector's recent sync errors: distinct redacted messages with `errorClass`, `count`, `firstSeenAt`, `lastSeenAt`, and `step`. Per the API description the counts come from a bounded scan, not an exhaustive log count.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors sync-errors <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--top-k int` | Maximum distinct error messages. Max `20` (the server's documented bound); a larger value is clamped to `20`, a negative one exits `2`. `0` or unset leaves the server's default. |

Output is the API's response object as returned.

### `connectors sync-changes`

Show a timeline of configuration, release, and deployment changes for a connector. Per the API description it is metadata only — changed field names, never values — oldest first, capped to the most recent 100 events in the window. `.truncatedSources` names any source that was incomplete.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors sync-changes <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--since string` | Start of the window, an RFC 3339 timestamp such as `2026-01-01T00:00:00Z`. Unset, the server chose the 7 days before now in the run checked. |

A `--since` in the future was a `400 timeline: since is in the future` in the run checked, and a value that isn't a timestamp is a `400`; both exit `2`. Output is the API's response object as returned.

### `connectors sync-compare`

Compare the recorded aggregate counts of two production sync runs of a connector: `--left` is the before run and `--right` the after run, both `lifecycleId` values from [`sync-history`](#connectors-sync-history).

The response carries `.perType` (resource, entitlement, and grant before, after, and delta per resource type, counts as JSON strings), `.typesAdded`, `.typesRemoved`, `.largeDecrease` (types whose count fell by at least 30 percent, per the API description), and `.c1zSizeDelta`. It holds counts only, never synced records. Comparing a run with itself was accepted in the run checked, with every delta `0`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors sync-compare <connector-id> --app-id <app-id> \
  (--left <lifecycle-id> --right <lifecycle-id> | --body-file <file>)
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--left string` | Lifecycle ID of the before run. (Required unless `--body-file`.) |
| `--right string` | Lifecycle ID of the after run. (Required unless `--body-file`.) |
| `--body-file string` | Full `CompareSyncStats` request JSON object (file, or `-` for stdin). Replaces `--left` and `--right`, for example to compare against a draft test run. |

The API also compares a run against a draft test run of a tenant-authored connector: send that request with `--body-file` using the `draftTestRun` object (`catalogId`, `revisionId`, `testRunId`). That form was not exercised live.

### `connectors credentials get`

Get a connector credential. Output is the credential itself, not the API's `credential` wrapper. `.clientSecret` is `""` here; only [`credentials rotate`](#connectors-credentials-rotate) returns it.

There is no command that lists a connector's credentials. A credential ID comes from the output of `credentials rotate`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors credentials get <credential-id> \
  --app-id <app-id> --connector-id <connector-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--connector-id string` | Connector ID. (Required.) |

The server resolves the credential ID globally and ignores the app and connector path segments, so c1i refuses a credential whose `appId` or `connectorId` differs from `--app-id` and `--connector-id` (exit `4`).

A revoked credential is still returned, at exit `0`: in the run checked, revoke set `.expiresTime` to a time in the past and left `.deletedAt` null.

### `connectors credentials rotate`

Issue a new client credential for a connector. The secret is shown once.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors credentials rotate \
  --app-id <app-id> --connector-id <connector-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--connector-id string` | Connector ID. (Required.) |

The new credential prints as pretty JSON, unwrapped like `credentials get`: `.id` sits beside `.clientId` and `.clientSecret`. Capture `.clientSecret` immediately; it can't be read again.

In the run checked, the previous credential stayed valid with `.expiresTime` set seven days ahead; revoke it with [`credentials revoke`](#connectors-credentials-revoke) to end it sooner. A self-hosted connector authenticates with this credential and keeps using the old one until it is given the new one.

### `connectors credentials revoke`

Revoke a connector credential. In the run checked, the credential stayed readable with `.expiresTime` set to a time in the past, and revoking it again exited `0`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i connectors credentials revoke <credential-id> \
  --app-id <app-id> --connector-id <connector-id>
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--app-id string` | Application ID. (Required.) |
| `--connector-id string` | Connector ID. (Required.) |

Prints `{}`; exit `0` means success.

## Policies

Manage policies — the objects that describe how C1 processes a task (an access request, a certification, a provisioning action): who approves it, what happens on escalation or timeout, and how the underlying resource gets provisioned.

| Command | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| [`c1i policies list`](#policies-list) | List all policies. |
| [`c1i policies search`](#policies-search) | Search policies by display name, description, type, or deletion state. |
| [`c1i policies get`](#policies-get) | Get a single policy by ID. |
| [`c1i policies create`](#policies-create) | Create a new policy. |
| [`c1i policies update`](#policies-update) | Update a policy. |
| [`c1i policies delete`](#policies-delete) | Soft-delete a policy by ID. |
| [`c1i policies validate-cel`](#policies-validate-cel) | Check a CEL condition for compile errors. |
| [`c1i policies test-provision`](#policies-test-provision) | Evaluate a CEL expression with the account provision policy tester. |

<Warning>
Creating or updating a policy with an empty (or missing) steps list succeeds with no validation error, and silently produces a deny-everything policy. The `create` and `update` commands refuse to send a request with empty or missing steps unless you pass `--allow-deny-all` to confirm that's what you want.
</Warning>

A policy's shape is deeply nested: `policySteps` holds an ordered list of steps, each one of seven types (approval, provision, accept, reject, wait, form, action), and an approval step's approver is itself one of several kinds (users, manager, group, app owners, self, entitlement owners, expression, webhook, resource owners, agent). You can read back all seven types, but `create` and `update` refuse two of them client-side with exit code 2, before sending a request: `a "provision" step is never allowed in a policy body — it's read-only and server-computed` and `an "action" step is not supported by create/update yet`. Modeling the nested structure as flags would be unusable, so `create` and `update` take the nested pieces from a JSON file (or `-` for stdin), while the flat top-level fields (display name, description, policy type) stay as flags.

Both `create` and `update` take their JSON from `--steps-file` (an array of step objects), `--rules-file` (an array of routing rules), or `--body-file` (the complete request object). Only one of the three can read stdin (`-`); two exit with code 2 before reading.

Every check c1i makes on those files reads a field in either spelling the API accepts (`policyId` or `policy_id`), nested fields included. A file that sets a field c1i checks in both spellings exits with code 2 before anything is sent, because the API refuses any field sent in both with a `400 duplicate field`.

### `policies list`

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits).

Unlike most list commands, this takes no query filter at all — only pagination. Use `search` for filtering.

Rows carry `step_kinds` (the kind of each step in the policy's baseline sequence) and `baseline_policy_id`, which together identify a tenant's auto-approval policy without matching on its display name. Search rows carry the same keys.

`step_count` counts every step in every sequence, including alternative ones, so it can exceed `step_kinds`' length. `baseline_policy_id` is non-null when a policy defers its baseline to another policy instead of holding one; `step_kinds` is then empty for a healthy policy, and `rule_count` tells a conditionally routed policy (`> 0`) from a plain one.

### `policies search`

[Paginates](#pagination-and-limits); `--page-size` defaults to 25.

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--query string` | Fuzzy search on display name and description. |
| `--display-name string` | Exact-ish (case-insensitive) display name match. |
| `--policy-type stringArray` | Filter by policy type: `grant`, `revoke`, `certify`, `access-request`, `provision`, or any `POLICY_TYPE_*` value (repeatable). |
| `--include-deleted` | Include soft-deleted policies. This is the only listing command that can find one. |
| `--exclude-policy-id stringArray` | Policy ID to exclude from results (repeatable). |

### `policies get`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i policies get <policy-id>
```

Unlike `list` and the default `search`, `get` returns a soft-deleted policy too (with `deletedAt` populated) rather than filtering it out. It is a verbatim passthrough of the API object, so it carries `policySteps` and `baselinePolicyId` but neither of the derived keys `list` and `search` add.

### `policies create`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i policies create --display-name <name> --policy-type <type> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--display-name string` | Display name for the new policy. Can't be blank: empty, whitespace, or invisible-only. (Required unless `--body-file`.) |
| `--description string` | Description for the new policy. |
| `--policy-type string` | Policy type: `grant`, `revoke`, `certify`, `access-request`, `provision`, or any `POLICY_TYPE_*` value. `access-request` is deprecated and `provision` is server-internal. (Required unless `--body-file`.) |
| `--steps-file string` | JSON array: the baseline `policySteps.steps` content (file, or `-` for stdin). The CLI wraps it under the lowercase type word (`grant`, `revoke`, `certify`), not the enum name. |
| `--rules-file string` | JSON array of routing rules: `[{"condition":"<CEL>","stepKey":"<key>"}]` (file, or `-` for stdin). A baseline or catch-all rule needs the literal condition `true`; an empty condition is a `400`. |
| `--body-file string` | Full create-policy request body, verbatim (file, or `-` for stdin); mutually exclusive with the flags above. |
| `--allow-deny-all` | Allow creating a policy with no steps (or an explicit deny-all). |

CEL conditions run with the root variable `subject`, not `user` — validate one with `c1i policies validate-cel` before using it here. That command checks the `rules[].condition` environment, which differs from the one an approval step's expression approver runs in.

### `policies update`

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i policies update <policy-id> [flags]
```

| Flag | Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| `--display-name string` | New display name. Omit to leave unchanged; can't be blank: empty, whitespace, or invisible-only. |
| `--description string` | New description; `""` clears it. |
| `--policy-type string` | New policy type: `grant`, `revoke`, `certify`, `access-request`, `provision`, or any `POLICY_TYPE_*` value (rarely changed). |
| `--steps-file string` | JSON array: replaces the baseline `policySteps.steps` content (file, or `-` for stdin). |
| `--rules-file string` | JSON array: replaces `rules[]` (file, or `-` for stdin). |
| `--body-file string` | Full policy JSON object, verbatim (file, or `-` for stdin); requires `--update-mask`; must not contain `id` (or must equal `<policy-id>`). |
| `--update-mask string` | Comma-separated field paths to update (required with `--body-file`; rejected without it; derived otherwise). |
| `--allow-deny-all` | Allow leaving or setting a policy with no steps. |

Only the fields you pass are changed — this is a partial update, not a full replacement (unless you use `--body-file`). A blank `--display-name` exits with code 2 before sending.

Some guards depend on `policyType`. If your patch changes `policySteps` but doesn't state `policyType`, the command reads the policy's current type first so those guards still run; a failed lookup surfaces as its own exit code, and `--policy-type` skips the read. `--dry-run` does the read too, so the preview body is exact.

### `policies delete`

The row is retained with `deletedAt` set — this is not a hard delete, and the policy still returns from `get` afterward. It disappears from `list` and the default `search`; `search --include-deleted` is the only listing that finds it.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i policies delete <policy-id>
```

A policy referenced by another policy (as a rule's outcome, or as another policy's baseline) can't be deleted while that reference exists — the server returns `400` naming the referencing policy, so no dangling reference is left behind.

### `policies validate-cel`

It creates and updates nothing, so you can test a rule's `condition` before using it in `create` or `update`.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i policies validate-cel [--] <condition>
```

**Example:**

```bash
c1i policies validate-cel 'subject.role == "admin"'
```

Quote the condition as a single argument, or pass several unquoted words; they are joined with spaces either way. Put `--` before a condition that starts with `-` (such as `-1 + 1`), or the flag parser reads it as a flag.

The response has no `valid` boolean; success is an empty `markers` array, and each compile error becomes one marker with severity `ERROR` and a line/column. The command adds a synthesized `valid` field derived from that. It exits `0` for a valid condition, `2` for an invalid one — so `c1i policies validate-cel '<cond>' && ...` only continues past a condition that compiles.

<Note>
This validates the CEL environment rules run in (`subject`, `account`, `entitlement`, `task`) — `user` is not declared there. It is not the environment an approval step's `expression` approver runs in (`subject`, `app_owners`/`appOwners`, `entitlement`, `task`, with no `account`). An expression that's valid in one may not compile in the other.
</Note>

### `policies test-provision`

Evaluate a CEL expression with the account provision policy tester and print the result: `value` (a string) and `type` (the result's data type). Nothing is created or changed.

**Usage:**

```bash
c1i policies test-provision [--] <cel>
```

**Example:**

```bash
c1i policies test-provision '1+1'
```

Quote the expression as one argument, or pass several unquoted words; they are joined with spaces, as in `validate-cel`. Put `--` before an expression that starts with `-`.

An expression that doesn't compile is answered by the server with a `500` carrying the CEL error text, which exits `6`, not `2`: c1i does not check CEL itself. Use `policies validate-cel` for compile checks of rule conditions.

## Access profiles

Manage access profiles — the objects that control which entitlements are requestable, and by whom. Admins create them to grant birthright access or to make access requestable by a chosen audience.

The API calls this object a request catalog and routes it under `/api/v1/catalogs`, so its JSON keys and the IDs you pass here say "catalog" — the two names refer to the same object. Not to be confused with an app catalog, the per-user list of what one user can request, derived from the access profiles they belong to.
